TL;DR: LoPE (Lorem Perturbation for Exploration) is a training framework that prepends random Lorem Ipsum text to prompts during GRPO reinforcement learning to break the "zero-advantage problem" in LLM reasoning, outperforming standard resampling across 1.7B to 7B models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO suffers from the "zero-advantage problem" where all sampled rollouts for a query fail, causing zero relative advantage and no effective training signal, which wastes data and computation. Simply increasing sampling budget is insufficient due to static sampling policy constraints.

Method: LoPE prepends stochastically assembled sequences from Lorem Ipsum vocabulary (pseudo-Latin placeholder text) to prompts before resampling, introducing task-irrelevant prompt-space perturbations that shift the model's output distribution to unlock orthogonal reasoning pathways.

Result: LoPE significantly outperforms resampling with original prompts across 1.7B, 4B, and 7B models. Other Latin-based random sequences with low perplexity also serve as effective perturbations.

Conclusion: LoPE establishes a strong baseline for broadening exploration in LLM reinforcement learning by breaking the exploration bottleneck caused by the zero-advantage problem.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, particularly Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has significantly advanced the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, in complex tasks, GRPO frequently suffers from the ``zero-advantage problem'': when all sampled rollouts for a query fail, the relative advantage collapses to zero. Consequently, the model loses effective training signals for these questions, wasting the training data and computational budget. While simply increasing the sampling budget for these questions is a common remedy, the static sampling policy inherently constrains reasoning exploration, limiting the success rate. In this paper, we propose Lorem Perturbation for Exploration (LoPE), a simple yet effective training framework to break this exploration bottleneck. We posit that task-irrelevant prompt-space perturbations can shift the model's output distribution enough to unlock orthogonal reasoning pathways for hard questions. Specifically, LoPE prepends sequences stochastically assembled from Lorem Ipsum vocabulary (a pseudo-Latin placeholder text) to the prompts before resampling. Experiments across 1.7B, 4B, and 7B models demonstrate that LoPE significantly outperforms resampling with the original prompts. Further analysis reveals that other Latin-based random sequences with low perplexity are also effective perturbations. Our results establish LoPE as a strong baseline for broadening exploration in LLM reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [54] [Locality-aware Private Class Identification for Domain Adaptation with Extreme Label Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05567)
*Chuan-Xian Ren,Cheng-Jun Guo,Hong Yan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ReOT, an optimal transport-based method for domain adaptation with private classes (classes present in only one domain). It introduces a locality-aware score function to identify private class samples and uses reliable transport to avoid mismatching between shared and private samples, with theoretical guarantees and strong empirical results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing domain adaptation methods assume private classes have sufficiently large distribution shifts to be detected as outliers, but this assumption fails when intra-class variance in shared classes exceeds inter-class differences between private and shared classes. This makes private class identification unreliable and degrades cross-domain classification performance.

Method: The method (ReOT) proposes a locality-aware private class identification score function based on local transportation and metric properties of optimal transport (OT). It then minimizes classification risk while learning separated cluster structures between identified shared and private classes, using reliable OT to transport knowledge intra-class and avoid shared-private mismatches.

Result: Theoretical proof of the score function's ability to distinguish shared vs. private class samples; a generalization upper bound for target risk under extreme label shift is provided; extensive experiments on benchmarks validate ReOT's effectiveness.

Conclusion: The locality-aware OT-based approach reliably handles private classes in domain adaptation even when intra-class variance challenges traditional outlier assumptions, with theoretical guarantees and practical superiority demonstrated.

Abstract: Domain adaptation aims to transfer knowledge from a labeled source domain to an unlabeled target domain with different distributions. In real-world scenarios, the label spaces of the two domains often have an inclusion relationship, where some classes exist only in one domain but not the other. These non-overlapping classes are referred to as private classes. Identifying private class samples and mitigating their adverse effects is critical in the literature. Existing methods rely on the assumption that shifts in private classes are large enough to be considered outliers. However, the variance within a single shared class can be significantly larger than the difference between a private class and another shared class, challenging this assumption. Consequently, private classes substantially increase the difficulty of cross-domain classification. To address these issues, based on local transportation and metric properties of optimal transport (OT), a locality-aware private class identification approach is proposed in the form of a score function on transport mass. The effectiveness of the proposed approach is theoretically proven, highlighting the score function's strong ability to distinguish between shared and private class samples. Building on this, we introduce a reliable OT-based method (ReOT) for domain adaptation under severe label shift. ReOT minimizes classification risk while learning the separated cluster structure between the identified shared classes and private classes, effectively avoiding mismatch between shared-private sample pairs, thus ensuring that important knowledge is reliably transported intra-class to mitigate class-conditional discrepancy. Furthermore, a generalization upper bound of the target risk is provided for extreme label shift scenarios, which can be minimized by ReOT. Extensive experiments on benchmarks validate the effectiveness of ReOT.

</details>


### [55] [AlphaCrafter: A Full-Stack Multi-Agent Framework for Cross-Sectional Quantitative Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05580)
*Yishuo Yuan,Jiayi Sheng,Sirui Zeng,Jiaqi Wang,Jiaheng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AlphaCrafter is a full-stack multi-agent framework with three specialized agents (Miner, Screener, Trader) that continuously adapts the factor-to-execution pipeline for quantitative trading, outperforming baselines on CSI 300 and S&P 500.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing quantitative trading frameworks treat factor discovery, regime selection, and execution as static or isolated components, lacking a fully automated, rationality-driven pipeline that adapts to non-stationary market conditions.

Method: AlphaCrafter uses three specialized agents: a Miner (LLM-guided continuous factor search), a Screener (regime-conditioned factor ensemble construction), and a Trader (risk-constrained strategy execution), forming a closed-loop cross-sectional trading system.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on CSI 300 and S&P 500 in risk-adjusted returns with the lowest cross-trial variance.

Conclusion: An integrated and adaptive factor-to-execution design produces robust trading performance by continuously coupling factor discovery, regime adaptation, and risk-constrained execution.

Abstract: Financial markets are inherently non-stationary, driven by complex interactions among macroeconomic regimes, microstructural frictions, and behavioral dynamics. Building quantitative strategies that remain profitable demands the continuous coupling of factor discovery, regime-adaptive selection, and risk-constrained execution. Prevailing approaches, however, optimize these components under static or isolated assumptions. Factor mining frameworks typically treat alpha discovery as a one-time search process, implicitly assuming that factor efficacy persists across market regimes. Execution-oriented systems often adopt role-playing agent architectures that simulate anthropomorphic trading committees, introducing behavioral noise rather than systematic rationality. Consequently, a fully automated, rationality-driven framework unifying a coherent quantitative pipeline remains absent. We introduce AlphaCrafter, a full-stack multi-agent framework that closes this gap through a continuously adaptive factor-to-execution pipeline, designed to track and respond to evolving market conditions without manual intervention. AlphaCrafter operates via three specialized agents: a Miner that continuously expands the factor pool via LLM-guided search, a Screener that assesses prevailing market conditions to construct regime-conditioned factor ensembles, and a Trader that translates these ensembles into quantitative strategies under explicit risk constraints. Together, these three agents form a closed-loop cross-sectional trading system that adapts holistically to evolving market dynamics. Extensive experiments on CSI 300 and S&P 500 demonstrate that AlphaCrafter consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in risk-adjusted returns while exhibiting the lowest cross-trial variance, confirming that integrated and adaptive factor-to-execution design yields robust trading performance.

</details>


### [56] [Belief Memory: Agent Memory Under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05583)
*Junfeng Liao,Qizhou Wang,Jianing Zhu,Bo Du,Rui Yan,Xiuying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BeliefMem is a probabilistic memory framework for LLM agents that stores multiple candidate conclusions with probabilities instead of a single deterministic conclusion, updating beliefs via Noisy-OR rules to handle partial observations and reduce self-reinforcing errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing external memory methods for LLM agents commit to a single deterministic conclusion per observation, discarding uncertainty and causing self-reinforcing errors where agents act on stored conclusions without revisiting alternatives.

Method: BeliefMem stores candidate conclusions as separate memory entries with probabilities updated via Noisy-OR rules as new observations arrive; all candidates surface together with probabilities at retrieval, preserving uncertainty.

Result: On LoCoMo and ALFWorld benchmarks, BeliefMem achieves the best average performance, substantially outperforming well-known baselines even with limited data.

Conclusion: Probabilistic memory provides substantial gains and opens a new direction for agent memory design in partially observable environments.

Abstract: LLM agents that operate over long context depend on external memory to accumulate knowledge over time. However, existing methods typically store each observation as a single deterministic conclusion (e.g., inferring "API~X failed" from temporary errors), even though such observations are inherently partial and potentially ambiguous. By committing to one conclusion and discarding uncertainty, these methods introduce self-reinforcing error: the agent acts on the stored conclusion, never revisits alternatives, and reinforces the conclusion over time. To address this issue, we propose BeliefMem, which shifts the memory paradigm from committing to a single conclusion per observation to retaining multiple candidate conclusions with their probabilities. Concretely, BeliefMem stores the candidate conclusions as separate memory entries, each carrying a probability that is updated via Noisy-OR rules as new observations arrive. At retrieval, all candidates surface together with their probabilities, keeping alternatives visible to the agent. Since each conclusion in memory retains its probability, BeliefMem preserves the uncertainty that the deterministic paradigm discards, enabling the agent to act with high confidence on well-evidenced knowledge while retaining the capacity to update its confidence when new evidence arrives. Empirical evaluations on LoCoMo and ALFWorld benchmarks show that, even with limited data, BeliefMem achieves the best average performance, remarkably outperforming well-known baselines. More broadly, such probabilistic memory produces substantial gains and explores a new direction for agent memory in partially observable environments.

</details>


### [57] [Causal Probing for Internal Visual Representations in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05593)
*Zehao Deng,Tianjie Ju,Zheng Wu,Liangbo He,Jun Lan,Huijia Zhu,Weiqiang Wang,Zhuosheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a causal framework using activation steering to probe and manipulate visual representations in MLLMs. It reveals that entities are encoded locally while abstract concepts are distributed globally, linking this divergence to scaling laws. It also uncovers a compensatory mechanism between perception and generation, and exposes a disconnect between perception and reasoning in visual tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap in understanding how Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) encode and ground distinct visual concepts internally.

Method: A causal framework based on activation steering to actively probe and manipulate internal visual representations across four visual concept categories.

Result: Entities exhibit localized memorization while abstract concepts are globally distributed; increasing model depth is crucial for abstract concepts but not for entities; reverse steering reveals a compensatory mechanism between perception and generation; visual reasoning exposes a disconnect where MLLMs treat geometric relations as static features rather than procedural reasoning triggers.

Conclusion: The divergence in concept encoding mechanistically drives scaling laws, and there are fundamental disconnects between perception, generation, and reasoning in MLLMs.

Abstract: Despite the remarkable success of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across diverse tasks, the internal mechanisms governing how they encode and ground distinct visual concepts remain poorly understood. To bridge this gap, we propose a causal framework based on activation steering to actively probe and manipulate internal visual representations. Through systematic intervention across four visual concept categories, our results reveal a divergence in concept encoding: entities exhibit distinct localized memorization, whereas abstract concepts are globally distributed across the network. Critically, this divergence uncovers a mechanistic driver of scaling laws: increasing model depth is indispensable for encoding distributed and complex abstract concepts, whereas entity localization remains remarkably invariant to scale. Furthermore, reverse steering uncovers that blocking explicit output triggers a surge in latent activations, exposing a compensatory mechanism between perception and generation. Finally, extending our analysis to visual reasoning, we expose a disconnect between perception and reasoning although MLLMs successfully recognize geometric relations, they treat them merely as static visual features, failing to trigger the procedural execution necessary for abstract problem-solving.

</details>


### [58] [Text-Graph Synergy: A Bidirectional Verification and Completion Framework for RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05643)
*Jiarui Zhong,Hong Cai Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TGS-RAG is a unified Text-Graph Synergistic enhancement framework that introduces bidirectional communication between text and graph modalities for Retrieval-Augmented Generation, using a Global Voting strategy to refine textual evidence and a Memory-based Orphan Entity Bridging algorithm to resurrect valid pruned reasoning paths, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines on multi-hop reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG approaches suffer from two key limitations: text-based RAG retrieves logically irrelevant pseudo-evidence, graph-based RAG discards potentially valid reasoning paths due to search-time pruning, and hybrid methods are hindered by the "Information Island" problem caused by asymmetric reasoning flows between unstructured text and structured graphs.

Method: TGS-RAG proposes a bidirectional Text-Graph synergistic framework with two channels: (1) Graph-to-Text channel uses a Global Voting strategy based on visited graph nodes to re-rank and refine textual evidence, filtering semantic noise; (2) Text-to-Graph channel employs a Memory-based Orphan Entity Bridging algorithm that uses textual cues to proactively resurrect valid but pruned reasoning paths from search history without additional database overhead.

Result: On multiple multi-hop reasoning benchmarks, TGS-RAG significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving a superior balance between retrieval precision and computational efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed bidirectional Text-Graph synergy effectively addresses the "Information Island" problem in hybrid RAG, and the Memory-based Orphan Entity Bridging algorithm enables recovery of valid reasoning paths without extra database costs, leading to improved retrieval precision and efficiency.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become a core paradigm for enhancing factual grounding and multi-hop reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). Traditional text-based RAG often retrieves logically irrelevant pseudo-evidence, while graph-based RAG is frequently hindered by search-time pruning, which may discard potentially valid reasoning paths. Existing hybrid approaches primarily adopt simple evidence concatenation or unidirectional enhancement, which fails to address the fundamental "Information Island" problem caused by asymmetric reasoning flows between unstructured text and structured graphs. We propose \textbf{TGS-RAG}, a unified framework for \textbf{T}ext-\textbf{G}raph \textbf{S}ynergistic enhancement. TGS-RAG introduces a bidirectional mechanism: (i) a \textbf{Graph-to-Text} channel that employs a Global Voting strategy from visited graph nodes to re-rank and refine textual evidence, filtering out semantic noise; and (ii) a \textbf{Text-to-Graph} channel that utilizes the \textbf{Memory-based Orphan Entity Bridging} algorithm. This algorithm utilizes textual cues to proactively resurrect valid but previously pruned reasoning paths from the search history without additional database overhead. Experimental results on multiple multi-hop reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that TGS-RAG significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving a superior balance between retrieval precision and computational efficiency.

</details>


### [59] [Proactive Instance Navigation with Comparative Judgment for Ambiguous User Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06223)
*Junhyuk Kwon,Seungjoon Lee,Hyejin Park,Kyle Min,Jungseul Ok*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProCompNav is a two-stage framework for instance navigation that uses comparative judgment to actively ask discriminative yes/no questions, narrowing candidate pools based on attribute-value splits, achieving higher success rates with shorter user responses on CoIN-Bench and state-of-the-art on TextNav.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing instance navigation agents either stop at the first plausible candidate without sufficient exploration or ask about attributes derived from individual candidates rather than questions that distinguish the entire candidate pool, leading to premature decisions and lengthy user responses.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) construct a candidate pool, then (2) identify the target via comparative judgment. At each round, an attribute-value pair is extracted that splits the current pool, a binary yes/no question is asked, and all inconsistent candidates are pruned at once, reframing disambiguation as pool-level discriminative questioning.

Result: On CoIN-Bench, ProCompNav improves Success Rate over both interactive baselines (same minimal input) and non-interactive baselines (detailed descriptions), while substantially reducing Response Length. Achieves state-of-the-art Success Rate on TextNav.

Conclusion: Comparative judgment is broadly useful for instance-level navigation among similar distractors, enabling more efficient and accurate target disambiguation.

Abstract: Natural-language instance navigation becomes challenging when the initial user request does not uniquely specify the target instance. A practical agent should reduce the user's burden by actively asking only the information needed to distinguish the target from similar distractors, rather than requiring a detailed description upfront. Existing approaches often fall short of this goal: they may stop at the first plausible candidate before sufficiently exploring alternatives, or, even after collecting multiple candidates, ask about the target's attributes derived from individual candidates rather than questions selected to distinguish candidates in the pool. As a result, despite the dialogue, the agent may still fail to distinguish the target from distractors, leading to premature decisions and lengthy user responses. We propose Proactive Instance Navigation with Comparative Judgment (ProCompNav), a two-stage framework that first constructs a candidate pool and then identifies the target through comparative judgment. At each round, ProCompNav extracts an attribute-value pair that splits the current pool, asks a binary yes/no question, and prunes all inconsistent candidates at once. This reframes disambiguation from open-ended target description to pool-level discriminative questioning, where each question is chosen to narrow the candidate set. On CoIN-Bench, ProCompNav improves Success Rate over interactive baselines with the same minimal input and non-interactive baselines with detailed descriptions, while substantially reducing Response Length. ProCompNav also achieves state-of-the-art Success Rate on TextNav, suggesting that comparative judgment is broadly useful for instance-level navigation among similar distractors.

</details>


### [60] [Retrieval-Conditioned Topology Selection with Provable Budget Conservation for Multi-Agent Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05657)
*Abhijit Talluri,Pujith Anne,Bhagavan Choudary Pendiyala,Raghavendra Chilukuri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RGAO is a multi-agent code generation system that adaptively selects orchestration topology based on code complexity using a hierarchical index, combined with a formal budget algebra to guarantee resource conservation, reducing misrouting from 30.1% to 8.2%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent LLM systems for code generation use fixed orchestration topologies regardless of the code's structural complexity, leading to suboptimal routing decisions. The paper identifies a fundamental routing problem: the optimal topology depends on the code under modification, but current systems select topologies without consulting the codebase.

Method: RGAO extracts a structural complexity vector from a hierarchical code index, then uses it to dynamically select the orchestration topology within Code-Agent, a multi-agent framework with formal contracts governed by six-dimensional budget vectors. The system combines complexity-conditioned LLM routing with formal resource algebras, featuring a complexity-conditioned topology router, a budget algebra with structural-induction conservation theorem, and a hierarchical code retrieval engine.

Result: Misrouting reduced from 30.1% to 8.2% (proxy-measured). Demonstrated sub-millisecond DAG construction and linear tree-index scalability.

Conclusion: RGAO achieves provable budget conservation under retrieval-conditioned dynamic topology selection by composing complexity-conditioned routing and formal resource algebras—a property neither approach achieves alone.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems for code generation face a fundamental routing problem: the optimal orchestration topology depends on the structural complexity of the code under modification, yet existing systems select topologies without consulting the codebase. We present Retrieval-Guided Adaptive Orchestration (RGAO), an architecture that closes this loop by extracting a structural complexity vector from a hierarchical code index before selecting the orchestration topology. RGAO operates within Code-Agent, a multi-agent framework whose sub-agents are governed by formal contracts with six-dimensional budget vectors. Our headline contribution is the composition of two previously separate lines of work -- complexity-conditioned LLM routing and formal resource algebras -- yielding a property neither admits alone: provable budget conservation under retrieval-conditioned dynamic topology selection. Concretely we contribute: (1) a complexity-conditioned topology router that reduces proxy-measured misrouting from 30.1% to 8.2%; (2) a budget algebra with a structural-induction conservation theorem; and (3) a hierarchical code retrieval engine. Empirical evaluation demonstrates sub-millisecond DAG construction and linear tree-index scalability.

</details>


### [61] [Large Vision-Language Models Get Lost in Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05668)
*Gongli Xi,Ye Tian,Mengyu Yang,Huahui Yi,Liang Lin,Xiaoshuai Hao,Kun Wang,Wendong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified information-theoretic and geometric framework to analyze residual updates in LVLMs, revealing that attention modules are subspace-preserving reconfiguration operators while FFNs are subspace-expanding semantic innovation drivers. It also shows that replacing learned attention weights with predefined values like Gaussian noise yields comparable or better performance, indicating redundancy and misallocation in current attention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing attribution-based analyses of LVLM internal modules lack a unified theoretical basis, making it difficult to systematically understand the distinct roles of attention and FFN modules within the residual-connection Transformer backbone.

Method: The authors develop a unified framework combining information theory and geometry to quantify the geometric and entropic properties of residual updates in decoder-only LVLMs, analyzing how attention and FFN modules affect representation subspaces.

Result: Attention is a subspace-preserving operator focused on reconfiguration, while FFNs are subspace-expanding operators driving semantic innovation. Replacing learned attention weights with predefined values (e.g., Gaussian noise) yields comparable or superior performance across most datasets.

Conclusion: Current LVLMs suffer from attention mechanism redundancy and misallocation—they "get lost in attention" rather than effectively leveraging visual context—suggesting the need for architectural optimization to reduce inefficiency.

Abstract: Despite the rapid evolution of training paradigms, the decoder backbone of large vision--language models (LVLMs) remains fundamentally rooted in the residual-connection Transformer architecture. Therefore, deciphering the distinct roles of internal modules is critical for understanding model mechanics and guiding architectural optimization. While prior statistical approaches have provided valuable attribution-based insights, they often lack a unified theoretical basis. To bridge this gap, we propose a unified framework grounded in information theory and geometry to quantify the geometric and entropic nature of residual updates. Applying this unified framework reveals a fundamental functional decoupling: Attention acts as a subspace-preserving operator focused on reconfiguration, whereas FFNs serve as subspace-expanding operators driving semantic innovation. Strikingly, further experiments demonstrate that replacing learned attention weights with predefined values (e.g., Gaussian noise) yields comparable or even superior performance across a majority of datasets relative to vanilla models. These results expose severe misallocation and redundancy in current mechanisms, suggesting that state-of-the-art LVLMs effectively ``get lost in attention'' rather than efficiently leveraging visual context.

</details>


### [62] [Attractor Geometry of Transformer Memory: From Conflict Arbitration to Confident Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05686)
*Qiyao Liang,Risto Miikkulainen,Ila Fiete*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language models have two failure modes: conflict (parametric and working memory disagree) and hallucination (fact never learned). Both produce confident outputs, but are distinguished by hidden-state geometry—conflict shows basin competition, hallucination shows basin absence. Geometric margin (distance to nearest memorized basin) detects hallucinations far better than output entropy, and this separation holds in both synthetic and natural-language settings. Confident hallucinations follow a scaling law and the frozen output head systematically erases epistemic state information, worsening with scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why language models produce confident but incorrect outputs in two distinct failure scenarios (conflict between memory sources and hallucination from missing knowledge), and to develop a better detection method than output-based monitoring, which is blind to these failures.

Method: The authors propose a unified geometric account: learned facts form attractor basins in hidden-state space. Conflict arises from basin competition (WM disrupts convergence), hallucination from basin absence (no memorized basin exists). They verify via synthetic tasks with LoRA adapters (causal isolation) and natural-language factual queries from pretrained models, using geometric margin as a detection metric.

Result: Geometric margin separates correct recall from hallucination far more cleanly than output entropy, with zero false refusals where entropy-based detection fails. The separation holds on natural-language queries without adaptation. Confident hallucinations follow a scaling law C = exp(-c/Δ̄), growing with scale even as overall error rates fall.

Conclusion: Hidden states reliably encode epistemic state, but the frozen output head systematically erases it—an erasure that worsens with model scale. Geometric approaches to monitoring are more effective than output-based methods for detecting hallucinations.

Abstract: Language models draw on two knowledge sources: facts baked into weights (parametric memory, PM) and information in context (working memory, WM). We study two mechanistically distinct failure modes--conflict, when PM and WM disagree and interfere; and hallucination, when the queried fact was never learned. Both produce confident output regardless, making output-based monitoring blind by design. We show both failures share a unified geometric account. In the hidden-state space of autoregressive generation, learned facts form attractor basins. Conflict is basin competition: WM disrupts convergence to the correct basin without raising output entropy. Hallucination is basin absence: the hidden state drifts freely when no memorized basin exists. The frozen LM head, designed for next-token prediction, cannot distinguish these cases and fires confidently either way. We verify this account in a controlled synthetic task--entity identifiers mapped to unique codes with PM installed via LoRA adapters--where ground truth is exact and component roles can be causally isolated through targeted adapter placement. Geometric margin--the hidden state's distance to the nearest memorized basin--reads this geometry directly and separates correct recall from hallucination far more cleanly than output entropy, with zero false refusals where entropy-based detection cannot avoid rejecting the vast majority of correct outputs. The separation holds on natural-language factual queries from the pretrained model with no adaptation, confirming attractor geometry is structural rather than a fine-tuning artifact. The fraction of confident hallucinations follows a scaling law $C = \exp(-c/\barΔ)$, growing with scale even as overall error rates fall. Hidden states reliably encode epistemic state; the frozen output head systematically erases it--and this erasure worsens with scale.

</details>


### [63] [DataDignity: Training Data Attribution for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05687)
*Xiaomin Li,Andrzej Banburski-Fahey,Jaron Lanier*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FakeWiki, a benchmark for pinpoint provenance (identifying source documents supporting a model's response), and proposes ScoringModel (a supervised contrastive ranker) and SteerFuse (training-free activation-steering retrieval fusion). ScoringModel achieves 52.2% Recall@10 (vs 35.0% for baselines) across 9 LLMs and 5 query conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Auditing language model outputs requires identifying which source document supports the knowledge in a response—a task that goes beyond simple correctness. Existing methods struggle because they rely on lexical or topical overlap rather than actual evidential support.

Method: The authors create FakeWiki, a controlled benchmark of 3,537 fabricated Wikipedia articles with ground-truth provenance. They evaluate 7 retrieval baselines, a training-free activation-steering fusion method (SteerFuse), and a supervised contrastive provenance ranker (ScoringModel) trained with InfoNCE loss using in-batch, retrieval-mined, and anti-document negatives.

Result: ScoringModel improves mean Recall@10 from 35.0 (best retrieval baseline) to 52.2, winning 41/45 model-by-condition cells. SteerFuse is second-best without supervised training. On jailbreak-inspired queries, ScoringModel improves Recall@10 by 15.7 points over baselines.

Conclusion: Robust training data attribution requires evaluation settings that separate true answer support from topical or lexical resemblance. Both supervised ranking and activation-based fusion effectively address this challenge.

Abstract: Auditing language-model outputs often requires more than judging correctness: an auditor may need to identify which source document most likely supports the knowledge expressed in a response. We study this as pinpoint provenance: given a prompt, a target-model response, and a candidate corpus, rank the documents that best support the response. We introduce FakeWiki, a controlled benchmark of 3,537 fabricated Wikipedia-style articles designed to preserve ground-truth provenance while weakening lexical shortcuts. FakeWiki includes QA probes, source-preserving paraphrases, retro-generated variants, hard anti-documents that remain topically similar while removing answer-critical facts, and five query conditions: clean prompting plus four jailbreak-inspired transformations. We evaluate seven retrieval baselines, a training-free activation-steering retrieval-fusion method, SteerFuse, and a supervised contrastive provenance ranker, ScoringModel. ScoringModel maps response and document features into a shared space and is trained with InfoNCE using in-batch, retrieval-mined, and anti-document negatives. Across nine open-weight instruction-tuned LLMs and five query conditions, ScoringModel improves mean Recall@10 from 35.0 for the strongest retrieval baseline to 52.2, without inference-time fusion, and wins 41/45 model-by-condition cells. SteerFuse is usually second-best despite requiring no supervised training, showing that activation-space evidence can efficiently complement text retrieval. On jailbreak-inspired transformed queries, ScoringModel improves Recall@10 by 15.7 points on average over the best baseline. Overall, our work shows that robust training data attribution requires evaluation settings that separate true answer support from topical or lexical resemblance.

</details>


### [64] [GCCM: Enhancing Generative Graph Prediction via Contrastive Consistency Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05689)
*Shaozhen Ma,Wei Huang,Hanchen Wang,Dong Wen,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GCCM introduces a contrastive consistency objective with negative pairs and feature perturbation to prevent shortcut solutions in consistency training for diffusion-based graph prediction, achieving consistent improvements over deterministic predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing consistency training methods for graph prediction can suffer from a shortcut solution where the model ignores the noisy target and collapses into a purely deterministic predictor, undermining the benefits of generative modeling.

Method: GCCM proposes (1) a contrastive consistency objective that adds negative pairs to enforce separation between different targets beyond standard self-consistency, and (2) feature perturbation on input node/edge features to break identical conditioning and reduce trivial shortcut solutions.

Result: Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets show GCCM mitigates the shortcut solution and yields consistent performance improvements over deterministic predictors in graph prediction tasks.

Conclusion: Contrastive consistency with negative pairs and feature perturbation effectively prevents shortcut learning in consistency training, enabling more effective diffusion-based graph prediction.

Abstract: Conditional generative models, particularly diffusion-based methods, have recently been applied to graph prediction by modeling the target as a conditional distribution given the input graph, yielding competitive results compared to deterministic predictor. However, existing diffusion-based prediction methods typically require expensive iterative denoising at inference and often suffer from unstable sampling, which motivates recent efforts to reduce inference denoising steps and enable stable sampling via techniques such as consistency training. Despite this progress, we find that existing consistency training methods for graph prediction could potentially fall into a shortcut solution: the model may attempt to satisfy the self-consistency constraint by ignoring the noisy target (i.e., assigning it negligible weight), ultimately collapsing into a purely deterministic predictor. To mitigate such shortcut solution, we propose GCCM, a graph contrastive consistency model that goes beyond isolated pairwise matching between the same target at different noise levels by introducing negative pairs into a contrastive consistency objective. This adds an additional separation requirement, making the shortcut solution no longer trivially sufficient to satisfy the proposed objective. Moreover, we apply feature perturbation to the input node/edge features to break identical conditioning on the input graph, so that the shortcut no longer yields the same predictions across noise levels and becomes less attractive. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that GCCM mitigates the shortcut solution and yields consistent performance improvements in graph prediction compared to deterministic predictors.

</details>


### [65] [Saliency-Aware Regularized Quantization Calibration for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05693)
*Yanlong Zhao,Xiaoyuan Cheng,Huihang Liu,Baihua He,Xinyu Zhang,Harrison Bo Hua Zhu,Wenlong Chen,Li Zeng,Zhuo Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SARQC introduces a saliency-aware regularization term to PTQ calibration that keeps quantized weights close to original weights, improving generalization and downstream performance without extra inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PTQ methods minimize layer-wise reconstruction error on limited calibration data, which can cause quantized weights to diverge from original weights, increasing generalization risk and degrading downstream performance.

Method: SARQC augments standard PTQ objectives with a saliency-aware regularization term that penalizes deviation of quantized weights from original weights during calibration, integrating seamlessly into both scale search and Gram-based PTQ pipelines.

Result: Consistent improvements in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy on dense and Mixture-of-Experts LLMs, with no additional computational overhead during inference.

Conclusion: Saliency-aware regularization effectively mitigates generalization risk in PTQ, providing a unified enhancement for existing quantization methods.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is an effective approach for deploying large language models (LLMs) under memory and latency constraints. Most existing PTQ methods determine quantization parameters by minimizing a layer-wise reconstruction error on a predetermined calibration dataset, usually optimized via either scale search or Gram-based methods. However, from the perspective of generalization risk, existing calibration objectives of PTQ based only on empirical reconstruction error on limited or unrepresentative calibration data could move the quantized weights away from the original weights. This may cause the generalization risk to diverge, potentially degrading downstream performance. To address this issue, we propose \emph{Saliency-Aware Regularized Quantization Calibration} (SARQC) a unified framework that augments the standard PTQ objective with a saliency-aware regularization term. This term encourages quantized weights to stay close to the original weights during calibration, leading to improved generalization during inference. SARQC integrates seamlessly into existing PTQ pipelines, enhancing both scale search and Gram-based methods under a unified formulation. Extensive experiments on dense and Mixture-of-Experts LLMs demonstrate consistent improvements in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy, without additional computational overhead during inference.

</details>


### [66] [Inference-Time Budget Control for LLM Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05701)
*Zhengru Fang,Senkang Forest Hu,Zhonghao Chang,Yu Guo,Yihang Tao,Hongyao Liu,Mengzhe Ruan,Jun Huang,Yuguang Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a two-stage inference-time budget control method for LLM search agents in multi-hop QA, using Value-of-Information (VOI) scoring for search actions and selective evidence-grounded answer finalization, outperforming baselines under hard dual-budget constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM search agents face hard limits on both tool calls and generated tokens during inference, and better answers require explicit control over which search action gets the next budget unit and when to commit to a final answer.

Method: The method formulates two-stage inference-time budget control: (1) at search time, a controller assigns task-level Value-of-Information (VOI) scores to each feasible action (retrieval, decomposition, answer commitment) based on marginal task value per budget unit under current state and remaining dual budget; (2) after search, a selective evidence-grounded finalizer compares the trajectory answer with a refined candidate and rewrites only when residual error appears to be a low-risk answer-form error.

Result: Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, three LLM backbones, and four budget levels, the method yields positive aggregate gains over four audited baselines under the same hard dual-budget protocol. Ablations show search-time budget control (especially budget-dependent penalty) provides the main gain, while answer-time control helps mainly when the retrieval path is already adequate.

Conclusion: Inference-time budget control for LLM search agents should govern both how budget is spent during search and how the final answer is committed.

Abstract: LLM search agents increasingly rely on tools at inference time, but their trajectories are often constrained by hard limits on both tool calls and generated tokens. Under such dual budgets, better answers require not only stronger models, but also explicit control over which search action should receive the next budget unit and when the accumulated evidence is sufficient to commit a final answer. We study this problem in multi-hop question answering (QA) and formulate it as two-stage inference-time budget control. At search time, our controller assigns each feasible action a task-level Value-of-Information (VOI) score, defined as an operational estimate of marginal task value per unit budget under the current search state and remaining dual budget, and uses this score to choose among retrieval, decomposition, and answer commitment. After search, a selective evidence-grounded finalizer compares the trajectory answer with a refined candidate and rewrites only when the residual error appears to be a low-risk answer-form error. Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, three LLM backbones, and four budget levels, the method yields positive aggregate gains over four audited baselines under the same hard dual-budget protocol. Ablations show that search-time budget control, especially budget-dependent penalty, provides the main performance gain, while answer-time control helps mainly when the retrieval path is already adequate. These results suggest that inference-time budget control for LLM search agents should govern both how budget is spent during search and how the final answer is committed.

</details>


### [67] [Knowledge-Graph Paths as Intermediate Supervision for Self-Evolving Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05702)
*Huyu Wu,Jun Liu,Xiaochi Wei,Yan Gao,Yi Wu,Yao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A self-evolving search agent framework improves upon Search Self-Play (SSP) by using knowledge-graph paths to provide relational context for question construction and Waypoint Coverage Reward (WCR) for graded partial credit, achieving gains across seven QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving search agents reduce dependency on human-written training questions, but existing SSP framework suffers from invalid/unverifiable questions due to isolated answer entities and binary outcome rewards that discard useful partial-trajectory signals.

Method: (1) Ground question construction in LLM-guided knowledge-graph subgraphs to provide relational context. (2) Introduce Waypoint Coverage Reward (WCR) that gives graded partial credit based on overlap between entities on the construction path (factual bridges) and the Solver's search trajectory, while preserving full reward for correct answers.

Result: Across seven QA benchmarks and nine model configurations, the approach improves average scores over standard SSP in all configurations, with notable gains on multi-hop QA tasks.

Conclusion: Knowledge-graph paths can be reused as lightweight intermediate supervision to provide relational guidance and process feedback without additional task-specific human annotations or manually labeled process steps.

Abstract: Self-evolving search agents reduce reliance on human-written training questions by generating and solving their own search tasks. We build on Search Self-Play (SSP), a representative Proposer and Solver framework in which questions are generated and answered via multi-step search and reasoning. In practice, however, SSP faces two bottlenecks: the Proposer constructs questions from isolated answer entities without relational context, yielding many invalid or unverifiable questions in early self-play training, while the Solver receives only a binary outcome reward that discards useful signal from partially on-track search trajectories. We address both bottlenecks by reusing knowledge-graph paths as construction-derived intermediate supervision for both question construction and reward shaping. First, we ground question construction in LLM-guided knowledge-graph subgraphs, providing relational context for the Proposer. Second, we observe that constructing and solving a multi-hop question can involve overlapping intermediate entities: the factual bridges used to formulate the question may provide approximate waypoints for answering it. Exploiting this overlap, we introduce Waypoint Coverage Reward (WCR), which grants graded partial credit to incorrect Solver trajectories according to their coverage of entities on the construction path, while preserving full reward for correct answers. Across seven QA benchmarks and nine model configurations, our approach improves the average score over standard SSP in all configurations, including notable gains on multi-hop QA tasks. These results suggest that knowledge-graph paths can be reused as lightweight intermediate supervision, providing both relational guidance and process feedback without additional task-specific human annotations or manually labeled process steps.

</details>


### [68] [Resolving the bias-precision paradox with stochastic causal representation learning for personalized medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05706)
*Peisong Zhang,Manqiang Peng,Yuxuan Wu,Pawit Phadungsaksawasdi,Wesley Yeung,Ye Zhang,Trang Nguyen,Qiang Zhang,Nan Liu,Meng Wang,Kee Yuan Ngiam,Yih-Chung Tham,Ching-Yu Cheng,Tianfan Fu,Qingyu Chen,Rosemary Ke,Chang Li,Wenzhuo Yang,Zhenghao Lu,Chunyou Lai,Yu Zhang,Sheng Zhong,Hao Deng,Dianbo Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new stochastic alignment method (sMMD) resolves the bias-precision paradox in causal representation learning for individualized treatment effects, improving accuracy by up to 11.5% and enabling interpretable clinical decision support across two large ICU cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for estimating individualized treatment effects from longitudinal data suffer from a bias-precision paradox: reducing confounding bias suppresses clinically informative heterogeneity, degrading patient-specific predictions.

Method: Introduces sampling-based maximum mean discrepancy (sMMD), a stochastic alignment strategy that replaces global adversarial balancing with subset-level matching, instantiated in a counterfactual outcome prediction framework with attribution-grounded interpretability.

Result: Across two ICU cohorts (n=27,783), the method reduces error by up to 11.5%, substantially increases recall in high-risk tasks, and improves clinician accuracy by 14.7% while reducing decision time.

Conclusion: sMMD selectively preserves clinically decisive variables and outperforms clinicians-in-training and large language models, enabling interpretable, real-time clinical decision support.

Abstract: Estimating individualized treatment effects from longitudinal observational data is central to data-driven medicine, yet existing methods face a fundamental limitation: reducing confounding bias often suppresses clinically informative heterogeneity, degrading patient-specific predictions. Here, we identify this tension as a bias-precision paradox in causal representation learning and introduce sampling-based maximum mean discrepancy (sMMD), a stochastic alignment strategy that replaces global adversarial balancing with subset-level matching. We instantiate this approach in a framework for counterfactual outcome prediction with attribution-grounded interpretability. Across two large-scale ICU cohorts (n = 27,783), our framework improves accuracy under distribution shift, reducing error by up to 11.5% and substantially increasing recall in high-risk tasks. Mechanistic analyses show that sMMD selectively preserves clinically decisive variables. In human-AI evaluation, our method outperforms clinicians-in-training and large language models, and improves clinician accuracy by 14.7% while reducing decision time, enabling interpretable, real-time clinical decision support.

</details>


### [69] [Conceal, Reconstruct, Jailbreak: Exploiting the Reconstruction-Concealment Tradeoff in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05709)
*Md Farhamdur Reza,Richeng Jin,Tianfu Wu,Huaiyu Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Existing intent-obfuscation jailbreak attacks on MLLMs suffer from a reconstruction–concealment tradeoff; this paper proposes concealment-aware variant construction and keyword-related distractor images that exploit the model's own reconstruction ability to more effectively bypass safety mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Intent-obfuscation jailbreak attacks on MLLMs transform harmful queries into concealed multimodal inputs to bypass safety filters, but existing methods struggle with the tradeoff between hiding harmful intent and maintaining recoverability. The paper aims to systematically analyze this tradeoff and develop more effective attacks.

Method: The paper analyzes three black-box intent-obfuscation methods through a reconstruction lens, finding they poorly balance the tradeoff; character-removed variants achieve better balance. It proposes concealment-aware variant construction that greedily selects low-harmful-keyword-alignment, mutually diverse character-removed variants, instantiated via five modality-aware prompting strategies, plus keyword-related distractor images providing diverse contextual visual cues.

Result: Experiments on both closed-source and open-source MLLMs demonstrate that the proposed strategies significantly outperform strong baselines, revealing the vulnerability that a model's own reconstruction ability can be exploited to recover hidden harmful intent and produce unsafe outputs.

Conclusion: Character-removed variants with concealment-aware selection and keyword-related distractor images better balance the reconstruction–concealment tradeoff, exposing a new vulnerability where the model's own reconstruction capability is turned against its safety mechanisms.

Abstract: Intent-obfuscation-based jailbreak attacks on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) transform a harmful query into a concealed multimodal input to bypass safety mechanisms. We show that such attacks are governed by a \emph{reconstruction--concealment tradeoff}: the transformed input must hide harmful intent from safety filters while remaining recoverable enough for the victim model to reconstruct the original request. Through a reconstruction analysis of three representative black-box methods, we find that existing transformations struggle to balance this tradeoff, limiting their effectiveness. In contrast, we show that character-removed variants achieve a better balance. Building on this, we propose \emph{concealment-aware variant construction}, which greedily selects character-removed variants that are low in harmful-keyword alignment and mutually diverse, and instantiates them through five modality-aware prompting strategies. We further introduce \emph{keyword-related distractor images} that depict the harmful keyword in diverse contexts, providing more effective auxiliary visual context than generic distractor images. Experiments across closed-source and open-source MLLMs show the proposed strategies outperform strong baselines, revealing an underexplored vulnerability: a model's own reconstruction ability can be exploited to recover hidden harmful intent and produce unsafe responses.

</details>


### [70] [Decodable but Not Corrected by Fixed Residual-Stream Linear Steering: Evidence from Medical LLM Failure Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05715)
*Ming Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM hidden states can linearly decode failure signals (71.6% accuracy) but cannot use them for correction: five fixed steering families yield zero effect (Delta~0), while the same probe enables selective abstention (AUROC=0.610, beating all uncertainty baselines). Failure is representational entangled with task-critical computation, preventing correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether linearly decodable failure signals in LLM hidden states can be leveraged to correct those failures, specifically examining the classification-correction gap.

Method: - Identify OT: stable behavioral regime in medical QA (Jaccard>=0.81, 94% inter-annotator agreement) where models answer correctly under resampling but fail with extended chain-of-thought.\n- Linear decoding of OT at 71.6% balanced accuracy.\n- Test 5 families of fixed linear steering (29 configs, n=1,273) across architectures (Qwen2.5-7B) and domains (MMLU-STEM).\n- Analyze representational entanglement via overlap analysis, shared-direction steering, LEACE concept erasure, and per-instance probe-steering correlation.\n- Evaluate probe for selective abstention vs. 5 uncertainty baselines.

Result: - All fixed linear steering yields Delta~0 (identical null cross-architecture and cross-domain).\n- OT direction has 85-88% overlap with task-critical computation (specificity ratio <=0.152).\n- Non-targeted shared-direction steering damages accuracy (-12.1pp).\n- LEACE concept erasure damages accuracy (-3.6pp, p=0.01); 10 random erasures produce Delta=+0.3pp.\n- Per-instance probe-steering correlation: r=-0.002 (p=0.97).\n- Same probe enables selective abstention: held-out AUROC=0.610, exceeding all 5 uncertainty baselines (p=0.009).

Conclusion: Decodable failure structure supports post-generation reliability estimation (selective abstention) even when fixed linear steering cannot exploit it for correction due to representational entanglement with task-critical computation.

Abstract: Can linearly decodable failure signals in LLM hidden states be leveraged to correct those failures? We investigate this classification-correction gap via Overthinking (OT)--a stable behavioral regime (Jaccard >= 0.81, 94% inter-annotator agreement) in medical QA where models answer correctly under resampling yet fail in extended chain-of-thought. OT is linearly decodable at 71.6% balanced accuracy (p < 10^{-16}). Yet five families of fixed linear steering (29 configurations, n=1,273) all yield Delta ~= 0, with identical null results cross-architecture (Qwen2.5-7B) and cross-domain (MMLU-STEM). Three convergent lines of evidence suggest representational entanglement: the OT direction has 85-88% overlap with task-critical computation (specificity ratio <= 0.152); non-targeted shared-direction steering damages accuracy (-12.1pp); and LEACE concept erasure damages accuracy (-3.6pp, p=0.01), while 10 random erasures produce Delta=+0.3pp. The per-instance probe-steering correlation is r=-0.002 (p=0.97). Positively, the same probe enables selective abstention (held-out AUROC=0.610, exceeding all five uncertainty baselines, p=0.009): decodable failure structure supports post-generation reliability estimation even when the fixed linear steering family cannot exploit it for correction.

</details>


### [71] [Detecting Time Series Anomalies Like an Expert: A Multi-Agent LLM Framework with Specialized Analyzers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05725)
*Hyeongwon Kang,Jeongseob Kim,Jinwoo Park,Pilsung Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a multi-agent framework for time-series anomaly detection that decomposes anomaly analysis into specialized analyzers for different anomaly types, uses evidence-grounded detection and structured reporting, achieving state-of-the-art performance without requiring real anomaly segments as in-context examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based time-series anomaly detection methods use a single general-purpose model to directly infer anomalies, which limits controllability, interpretability, and reliability for complex anomaly patterns.

Method: SAGE decomposes anomaly analysis into four specialized Analyzers (point, structural, seasonal, pattern anomalies) using family-specific numerical tools and diagnostic visualizations. An evidence-grounded Detector consolidates evidence into confidence-scored records, and a Supervisor generates diagnostic reports. Synthetic in-context examples are constructed from normal-reference training segments without using real anomalous segments.

Result: Across three benchmarks, SAGE achieves the best average performance among strong ML/DL and language-model-based baselines. Ablation studies and human evaluation show improved detection reliability and practical usefulness.

Conclusion: The multi-agent structured decomposition approach improves controllability, interpretability, and reliability for time-series anomaly detection compared to single-model inference methods.

Abstract: Recent studies have explored large language models for time-series anomaly detection, yet existing approaches often rely on a single general-purpose model to directly infer anomaly indices or intervals, limiting controllability, interpretability, and reliability for complex anomaly patterns. We propose SAGE (Specialized Analyzer Group for Expert-like Detection), a multi-agent framework for structured anomaly diagnosis in univariate time series. It decomposes anomaly analysis into four specialized Analyzers for point, structural, seasonal, and pattern anomalies. Each Analyzer applies family-specific numerical tools and diagnostic visualizations to generate evidence, while an evidence-grounded Detector consolidates the evidence into confidence-scored anomaly records with intervals and candidate types. A Supervisor then converts these structured records into analyst-facing diagnostic reports. SAGE further constructs synthetic in-context examples from normal-reference training segments, without using real anomalous segments or anomaly-type labels as in-context examples. Across three benchmarks, SAGE achieves the best average performance among strong ML/DL and language-model-based baselines. Ablation studies and human evaluation further show that the proposed framework improves detection reliability and the practical usefulness of diagnostic outputs.

</details>


### [72] [SkillRet: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Skill Retrieval in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05726)
*Hongcheol Cho,Ryangkyung Kang,Youngeun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillRet is a large-scale benchmark for skill retrieval in LLM agents, containing 17,810 skills, 63,259 training samples, and 4,997 evaluation queries, showing that current retrievers perform poorly and fine-tuning yields significant gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents use large skill libraries, selecting the right skill becomes critical but underexplored. Existing benchmarks are limited, and the problem of skill retrieval under tight context/latency budgets lacks systematic study.

Method: The authors construct SkillRet with 17,810 public agent skills organized by a two-level taxonomy (6 major, 18 sub-categories) with structured semantic tags, providing 63,259 training samples and 4,997 evaluation queries with disjoint skill pools. They evaluate diverse retrievers and compare fine-tuned models against off-the-shelf ones.

Result: Off-the-shelf retrievers struggle on realistic large-scale skill libraries. Task-specific fine-tuning on SkillRet improves NDCG@10 by +13.1 points over the strongest prior retriever and +16.9 points over the strongest off-the-shelf retriever. Fine-tuned models better focus on small skill-relevant signals within long/noisy queries.

Conclusion: SkillRet establishes a strong benchmark for skill retrieval in large-scale agent systems, showing substantial headroom remains and providing a foundation for future research.

Abstract: As LLM agents are increasingly deployed with large libraries of reusable skills, selecting the right skill for a user request has become a critical systems challenge. In small libraries, users may invoke skills explicitly by name, but this assumption breaks down as skill ecosystems grow under tight context and latency budgets. Despite its practical importance, skill retrieval remains underexplored, with limited benchmarks and little understanding of retrieval behavior on realistic skill libraries. To address this gap, we introduce SkillRet, a large-scale benchmark for skill retrieval in LLM agents. SkillRet contains 17,810 public agent skills, organized with structured semantic tags and a two-level taxonomy spanning 6 major categories and 18 sub-categories. It provides 63,259 training samples and 4,997 evaluation queries with disjoint skill pools, enabling both benchmarking and retrieval-oriented training. Across a diverse set of retrievers, we find that skill retrieval remains far from solved: off-the-shelf models struggle on realistic large-scale skill libraries, and prior skill-retrieval models still leave substantial headroom. Task-specific fine-tuning on SkillRet substantially improves performance, improving NDCG@10 by +13.1 points over the strongest prior retriever and by +16.9 points over the strongest off-the-shelf retriever. Our analysis further suggests that these gains arise because fine-tuned models better focus on the small skill-relevant signals within long and noisy queries. These results establish SkillRet as a strong benchmark and foundation for future research on retrieval in large-scale agent systems.

</details>


### [73] [Knee Osteoarthritis Severity Grading Using Optimized Deep Learning and LLM-Driven Intelligent AI on Computationally Limited Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05731)
*Dayam Nadeem,Neha,Safdar Mustafa,Adnan Alvi,Mohd Hussain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An automated KOA severity grading system combining ResNet-18 CNN with TensorFlow Lite for on-device deployment and Gemini-2.0-flash LLM for interpretable diagnostic reporting, achieving 94.48% test accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KOA diagnosis suffers from subjectivity and inter-observer variability in conventional practices. Precise and timely diagnosis is needed for effective severity assessment, especially in resource-constrained settings without continuous internet connectivity.

Method: A ResNet-18 CNN model classifies knee X-ray images into five Kellgren-Lawrence (KL) grades via transfer learning. The model is optimized and converted to TensorFlow Lite format for on-device deployment. A Gemini-2.0-flash LLM generates structured interpretive findings (symptoms, risk factors, preventive measures) as an auxiliary interface layer without affecting classification.

Result: Test accuracy of 94.48% with stable convergence during training. The lightweight TensorFlow Lite model enables seamless deployment on resource-constrained devices operating without continuous internet connectivity.

Conclusion: The proposed model demonstrates the feasibility of an on-device, interpretable decision-support tool for early KOA diagnosis, improving accessibility to AI-assisted knee screening in low-connectivity environments.

Abstract: Knee osteoarthritis (KOA) is among the musculoskeletal disorders that considerably restrict joint mobility, cause severe chronic pain and impact negatively on quality life. It is one of the persistent health issues worldwide. Generally, subjectivity and inter-observer variability undermine conventional practices and evaluation process that are adopted to address such health issues. Hence precise and timely diagnosis would be one of the effective ways for the assessment of its severity. This paper proposes an automated diagnostic approach for severity grading of KOA by blending a deep learning convolutional neural network (CNN) with a device-based inference platform powered by TensorFlow Lite. It proposes a model based on the ResNet-18 convolutional neural network. The designed model is trained on publicly available database. Through a transfer learning approach obtained knee images are first classified into five Kellgren-Lawrence (KL) grades. Further the developed model is optimised. During the training of the model test accuracy of 94.48% with stable convergence has been achieved. Subsequently the optimised model transformed into a lightweight TensorFlow Lite format, facilitating seamless deployment on resource-constrained devices. The designed model is capable enough to operate in the environment having no continuous internet connectivity. Also, an auxiliary Large Language Model (Gemini-2.0-flash) is applied to generate structured interpretive findings like potential symptoms, risk factors, and preventive majors etc. The LLM component functions as interface without influencing the classification process. The proposed model articulates the feasibility of an on-device, interpretable decision-support tools for early diagnosis and improve accessibility to Artificial Intelligence (AI)-assisted knee screening tool.

</details>


### [74] [SDFlow: Similarity-Driven Flow Matching for Time Series Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05736)
*Wei Li,Shibo Feng,Pengcheng Wu,Min Wu,Peilin Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SDFlow is a non-autoregressive flow matching framework for time-series generation that operates in a frozen VQ latent space, eliminating exposure bias, reducing high-dimensionality via low-rank manifold decomposition, and incorporating discrete supervision into continuous flow matching. It achieves state-of-the-art performance with faster inference than autoregressive baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive (AR) token modeling in vector quantization (VQ) based time-series generation suffers from exposure bias, where errors accumulate during sequential inference, causing quality degradation in long-horizon generation. The authors aim to overcome this fundamental limitation.

Method: SDFlow proposes a non-autoregressive framework using flow matching in a frozen VQ latent space. It addresses three challenges: (1) replaces step-wise token prediction with a global transport map to eliminate exposure bias; (2) reduces VQ token space dimensionality via low-rank manifold decomposition with a learned anchor prior; (3) incorporates discrete supervision into continuous dynamics via a categorical posterior over codebook indices within a variational flow-matching formulation.

Result: SDFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving Discriminative Score and substantially reducing Context-FID, especially for long-sequence generation. It also provides significant inference speedups over autoregressive baselines.

Conclusion: SDFlow demonstrates that a non-autoregressive flow matching approach in VQ latent space can overcome exposure bias while delivering both high fidelity and computational efficiency for time-series generation, setting new state-of-the-art results.

Abstract: Vector quantization (VQ) with autoregressive (AR) token modeling is a widely adopted and highly competitive paradigm for time-series generation. However, such models are fundamentally limited by exposure bias: during inference, errors can accumulate across sequential predictions, leading to pronounced quality degradation in long-horizon generation. To address this, we propose SDFlow ($\textbf{S}$imilarity-$\textbf{D}$riven $\textbf{Flow}$ Matching), a non-autoregressive framework that operates entirely in the frozen VQ latent space and enables parallel sequence generation via flow matching. We tackle three key challenges in making this transition: (1) eliminating exposure bias by replacing step-wise token prediction with a global transport map; (2) mitigating the high-dimensionality of VQ token spaces via a low-rank manifold decomposition with a learned anchor prior over the latent manifold; and (3) incorporating discrete supervision into continuous transport dynamics by introducing a categorical posterior over codebook indices within a variational flow-matching formulation. Extensive experiments show that SDFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving Discriminative Score and substantially reducing Context-FID, particularly for challenging long-sequence generation. Moreover, SDFlow provides significant inference speedups over autoregressive baselines, offering both high fidelity and computational efficiency. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/SDFlow-D6F3/

</details>


### [75] [ReFlect: An Effective Harness System for Complex Long-Horizon LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05737)
*Fan Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReFlect is a deterministic harness system that wraps LLMs with standalone error detection and recovery logic, outperforming standard reasoning paradigms (CoT, ReAct, self-critique) on long-horizon multi-stage tasks. It achieves up to +29 pp gains over Direct CoT and raises SWE-bench patch quality from 0% to 87%, with gains inversely proportional to baseline success rates (slope=-1.69, r=-0.76).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reasoning paradigms (CoT, ReAct, post-hoc self-critique) rely on flawed assumptions that cause silent error accumulation on long-horizon, multi-stage tasks. There is an open question whether reasoning systems can effectively detect and recover from their own failures.

Method: ReFlect is a harness system that creates standalone error detection and recovery logic as a deterministic wrapper around the LLM. It operates at inference time, is model-agnostic and training-free.

Result: Prompt-level self-critique produces formulaic templates with 90/100 blocks flagging no issues; LLMs wrongly accept wrong answers in ≥76% of cases. ReFlect achieves task success rates from 41% (gpt-4o-mini) to 56% (Claude Sonnet 4.5), with per-model gains from +7 pp to +29 pp over Direct CoT. SWE-bench patch quality rises from 0% to 82-87%. Harness gain inversely correlates with baseline success rate (slope=-1.69, r=-0.76). Structured reasoning state fails on smaller models (15.0-18.7% pair-mean).

Conclusion: ReFlect demonstrates that a deterministic wrapper-based approach effectively detects and recovers from LLM reasoning failures in long-horizon tasks, with gains most pronounced for weaker baselines. Structured state reasoning approaches remain infeasible at smaller model scales.

Abstract: Current reasoning paradigms for LLMs include chain-of-thought, ReAct, and post-hoc self-critique. These paradigms rely on two assumptions that fail on long-horizon, multi-stage tasks. As a result, errors accumulate silently across reasoning steps, leaving an open question: can a reasoning system effectively detect and recover from its own failures? We present ReFlect, a \emph{harness} system for LLM reasoning that creates standalone error detection and recovery logic as a deterministic wrapper around the model. Controlled experiments across 6 reasoning domains show that prompt-level self-critique produces formulaic templates that flag no issues in 90 of 100 audited reflection blocks, and the investigated LLMs wrongly accept a wrong answer in at least 76\% of cases. Our ReFlect harness achieves task success rates ranging from 41\% on gpt-4o-mini to 56\% on Claude Sonnet 4.5 across six models spanning small and frontier scale, with per-model gains over Direct CoT ranging from +7 pp on Qwen2.5-72B to +29 pp on Claude Sonnet 4.5, and additionally raises SWE-bench patch-structural quality from 0\% (Direct CoT) to between 82\% (Qwen2.5-72B) and 87\% (GPT-4o). Notably, the harness gain is inversely proportional to the model's Direct CoT task success rate (the fitted slope is -1.69 with r=-0.76): each pp lost in baseline success rate is mechanically recovered by 1.69 pp of harness gain. We spot that adding structured reasoning state and operators yields only 15.0--18.7\% pair-mean on Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B because models at this scale cannot reliably populate the state its operators require. ReFlect is model-agnostic, training-free, and operates entirely at inference time.

</details>


### [76] [HyperLens: Quantifying Cognitive Effort in LLMs with Fine-grained Confidence Trajectory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05741)
*Chengda Lu,Xiaoyu Fan,Wei Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperLens is a high-resolution probe that traces confidence trajectories in transformer layers, revealing that deeper layers magnify small confidence changes. It introduces a cognitive effort metric showing complex tasks require more effort, and diagnoses that Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) can reduce cognitive effort, harming in-domain task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The inference dynamics of LLMs remain poorly understood due to limited resolution of existing analysis tools. The paper aims to understand how transformers process information across layers during inference.

Method: Identifies an intrinsic magnification mechanism in transformer architectures where deeper layers amplify small confidence changes. Builds HyperLens, a high-resolution probe to trace confidence trajectories and quantify cognitive effort during inference.

Result: HyperLens reveals consistent divergence in confidence trajectories separating complex from simple tasks. Complex tasks consistently require higher cognitive effort. SFT reduces cognitive effort, which degrades performance on in-domain tasks.

Conclusion: The magnification mechanism in transformers enables fine-grained analysis of inference dynamics. Cognitive effort is a fundamental metric for task complexity. SFT's side effect of reducing cognitive effort can explain performance degradation on in-domain tasks.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve strong performance across diverse tasks, their inference dynamics remain poorly understood because of the limited resolution of existing analysis tools. In this work, we identify an intrinsic magnification mechanism in transformer architectures: deeper layers inherently magnify the small changes of layer-wise confidence, providing a fine-grained confidence trajectory. Building on this insight, we introduce HyperLens, a high-resolution probe designed to trace confidence trajectories and quantify the cognitive effort during inference. Across LLMs and datasets, HyperLens reveals a consistent divergence in confidence trajectories that separates complex from simple tasks. We abstract this pattern into a quantitative cognitive effort metric. Our analysis reveals a fundamental principle: complex tasks consistently require higher cognitive effort. Finally, we provide a mechanistic diagnosis of a common side effect of standard Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT): it can reduce cognitive effort and consequently degrade performance on in-domain tasks.

</details>


### [77] [Best Arm Identification in Generalized Linear Bandits via Hybrid Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05745)
*Qirun Zeng,Xuchuang Wang,Jiayi Shen,Xutong Liu,Fang Kong,Jinhang Zuo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies fixed-confidence best arm identification in generalized linear bandits under a hybrid feedback model combining absolute rewards and relative (dueling) feedback. The authors propose a likelihood-ratio-based confidence sequence, a hybrid Track-and-Stop algorithm with minimax-optimal design, and extend to cost-aware settings, demonstrating improved sample efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
