Treatment planning is inherently a complex reasoning and refinement task, not a simple generation problem, thus requiring iterative revision like human experts.

Method: TheraAgent replaces one-shot generation with an iterative generate-judge-refine pipeline that mirrors human expert reasoning. It introduces TheraJudge, a treatment-specific evaluation module integrated into the inference loop to enforce clinical standards during the judge phase.

Result: Achieved state-of-the-art results on HealthBench in Accuracy and Completeness. Attained 86% win rate against physicians in expert evaluations with superior Targeting and Harm Control. TheraJudge showed high agreement with HealthBench evaluations.

Conclusion: The iterative generate-judge-refine framework with a dedicated evaluation module significantly improves treatment plan quality over one-shot LLM approaches and can match/exceed human physician performance, with reliable automated evaluation.

Abstract: Formulating a treatment plan is inherently a complex reasoning and refinement task rather than a simple generation problem. However, existing large language models (LLMs) mainly rely on one-shot output without explicit verification, which may result in rough, incomplete, and potentially unsafe treatment plans. To address these limitations, we propose TheraAgent, an agentic framework that replaces one-shot generation with an iterative generate-judge-refine pipeline. By mirroring the actual reasoning process of human experts who iteratively revise treatment plans, our framework progressively transforms coarse and incomplete drafts into precise, comprehensive, and safer therapeutic regimens. To facilitate the critical judge component, we introduce TheraJudge, a treatment-specific evaluation module integrated into the inference loop to enforce clinical standards. Experiments show TheraAgent achieves state-of-the-art results on HealthBench, leading in Accuracy and Completeness. In expert evaluations, it attains an 86% win rate against physicians, with superior Targeting and Harm Control. Moreover, the highly agreement between TheraJudge and HealthBench evaluations confirms the reliability of our framework.

</details>


### [104] [BehaviorGuard: Online Backdoor Defense for Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05977)
*Yinbo Yu,Xueyu Yin,Jiadai Wang,Chunwei Tian,Sai Xu,Qi Zhu,Daoqiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BehaviorGuard is an online, trigger-agnostic framework for detecting and mitigating backdoor attacks in DRL by monitoring behavioral drift in action distributions, outperforming prior methods across single- and multi-agent settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing backdoor defenses for DRL rely on reward anomaly detection and fine-tuning, which fail against complex trigger patterns and incur high computational costs, limiting practical deployment.

Method: BehaviorGuard detects backdoor behaviors by measuring consistent shifts in action distributions (high-quantile regions and distribution tails) without requiring trigger knowledge, using a novel behavioral drift metric to identify and suppress malicious actions online.

Result: Evaluated across diverse benchmarks and multiple backdoor attacks, BehaviorGuard consistently outperforms prior defenses in both detection efficacy and computational efficiency for both single- and multi-agent DRL.

Conclusion: Trigger-agnostic online behavioral monitoring is a viable and superior alternative to trigger-reverse-engineering and fine-tuning for backdoor defense in DRL, and BehaviorGuard is the first online defense effective against attacks in both single- and multi-agent settings.

Abstract: Backdoor attacks pose a serious threat to deep reinforcement learning (DRL). Current defenses typically rely on reward anomalies to reverse-engineer triggers and model finetuning to remove backdoors. However, complex trigger patterns undermine their robustness, and fine-tuning entails high costs, limiting practical utility. Therefore, we shift defense concerns to trigger-agnostic backdoor output behaviors and propose BehaviorGuard, an online behavior-based backdoor detection and mitigation framework for DRL. Specifically, we find that regardless of attacks, backdoored policies induce consistent shifts in action distributions to ensure reliable activation, leaving detectable traces in high-quantile regions and distribution tails, even in the absence of triggers. Based on this, we design a novel metric that captures behavioral drift in action distributions to identify and suppress backdoor actions at runtime. To our knowledge, this is the first online backdoor defense that counters attacks both in single- and multi-agent DRL. Evaluated across diverse benchmarks with different backdoor attacks, BehaviorGuard consistently surpasses prior methods in both efficacy and efficiency.

</details>


### [105] [TACT: Mitigating Overthinking and Overacting in Coding Agents via Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05980)
*Yuan Sui,Yulin Chen,Yibo Li,Xue Jiang,Yufei He,Yihong Dong,Xiaoxin He,Tianyu Gao,Bryan Hooi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TACT detects and mitigates agent drift (overthinking/overacting) in language model agents via activation steering in the residual stream, improving resolve rates by ~5pp and reducing steps by up to 26% on software engineering benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language model agents performing complex software engineering tasks degrade over long trajectories due to *agent drift*, manifesting as *overthinking* (repeated reasoning on known information) and *overacting* (issuing tool calls without integrating new observations). Prior methods lack targeted, online detection and correction of these failure modes before they surface behaviorally.

Method: The paper proposes TACT (Think-Act Calibration via activation Steering): (1) label trajectory steps as overthinking, overacting, or calibrated; (2) learn linear classifiers (drift axes) in the residual stream separating these classes (AUC ≈ 0.9); (3) at test time, project each step's hidden state onto these axes and pull drifted activations back toward the calibrated region via activation steering.

Result: TACT outperforms unsteered baselines on SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2.0, and CLAW-Eval. It lifts average resolve rate by +5.8 pp on Qwen3.5-27B and +4.8 pp on Gemma-4-26B-A4B-it, while cutting steps-to-resolve by up to 26%.

Conclusion: Agent drift is a steerable direction in the residual stream, and TACT provides a viable handle for building reliable long-horizon agents. The work frames behavioral failures in agentic loops as detectable and correctable via activation-space interventions.

Abstract: When language model agents tackle complex software engineering tasks, they often degrade over long trajectories, which we define as *agent drift*. We focus on two recurring failure modes *overthinking* and *overacting*, i.e., where the agent repeatedly reasons over information it already has, and where it issues tool calls without integrating recent observations or acquiring new evidence. In this paper, we introduce TACT (Think-Act Calibration via activation Steering), to detect and mitigate agent drift in the residual stream before it surfaces as a behavioral failure. In specific, we label trajectory steps as overthinking, overacting, or calibrated, and find that their hidden states can separate linearly along two *drift axes*, pointing from calibrated behavior toward each failure mode (AUC $\approx$ 0.9). To mitigate agent drift, we project each step's activation onto these axes at test time and pull drifted ones back toward the calibrated region. Experiments show that TACT outperforms unsteered baselines across SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2.0, and CLAW-Eval, lifting average resolve rate by $+5.8$ pp on Qwen3.5-27B and $+4.8$ pp on Gemma-4-26B-A4B-it while cutting steps-to-resolve by up to $26\%$. These gains frame agent drift as a steerable direction in the residual stream, and position TACT as a viable handle for reliable long-horizon agents.

</details>


### [106] [BioResearcher: Scenario-Guided Multi-Agent for Translational Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05985)
*Remigiusz Kinas,Joanna Krawczyk,Rafał Powalski,Przemysław Pietrzak,Agnieszka Kowalewska,Krzysztof Kolmus,Maciej Sypetkowski,Łukasz Smoliński,Tomasz Jetka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ingenix BioResearcher is a scenario-guided multi-agent system for translational medicine that maps queries to versioned playbooks, uses 30+ tools and ML endpoints, applies multi-model reconciliation, and achieves strong results across unit-level, open-ended, and end-to-end clinical evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Translational medicine requires evidence synthesis combining literature, trials, patents, and multi-omics analysis with provenance and uncertainty preservation, but existing foundation models and tool-augmented systems produce single-shot or open-ended answers that lack the auditable, scenario-specific workflows needed for heterogeneous biomedical data.

Method: A scenario-guided multi-agent system that maps queries to versioned research playbooks, delegates tasks to specialized subagents over 30+ tools and ML endpoints, integrates structured databases with sandboxed code for genome-scale analyses, and performs claim-level multi-model reconciliation before editorial assembly.

Result: 83.49% pass rate (0.892 avg score) on 109 single-step tests; 89.33% on BixBench-Verified-50; top 0.758 mean score on BaisBench Scientific Discovery; 74.7%±3.3% positive hit rate and 96.8%±0.2% negative clear rate on 30-query clinical end-to-end benchmark.

Conclusion: BioResearcher demonstrates broad, competitive performance across unit-level, open-ended, and end-to-end clinical evaluations, addressing the workflow and auditability gaps of general-purpose models for translational medicine.

Abstract: Translational medicine turns underspecified development goals into evidence synthesis that must combine literature, trials, patents, and quantitative multi-omics analysis while preserving identifiers, uncertainty, and retrievable provenance. General-purpose foundation models and off-the-shelf tool-augmented or multi-agent systems are not built for this: they tend to produce single-shot answers or run open-endedly, and fall short on the auditable, scenario-specific workflows that heterogeneous biomedical sources demand.
  This paper introduces Ingenix BioResearcher, a scenario-guided multi-agent system that maps queries to versioned research playbooks, delegates to specialized subagents over 30+ tools and machine-learning endpoints, mixes structured database access with sandboxed code for genome-scale analyses, and applies claim-level multi-model reconciliation before editorial assembly.
  We evaluate BioResearcher across unit-level capabilities, open-ended biomedical reasoning, and end-to-end clinical discovery. It leads evaluated baselines on 109 single-step tests (83.49% pass rate; 0.892 average score), achieves strong biomedical benchmark performance (89.33% on BixBench-Verified-50 and the top 0.758 mean score on BaisBench Scientific Discovery), and leads on a 30-query clinical end-to-end benchmark with the highest positive hit rate (74.7% $\pm$ 3.3%) and negative clear rate (96.8% $\pm$ 0.2%). These results show broad, competitive performance across unit-level, open-ended, and end-to-end clinical evaluations.

</details>


### [107] [Strat-LLM: Stratified Strategy Alignment for LLM-based Stock Trading with Real-time Multi-Source Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06024)
*Wenliang Huang,Zengyi Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Strat-LLM is a framework evaluating LLM-based trading agents across different strategy alignment modes (Free, Guided, Strict) in live-forward 2025 settings, finding that reasoning-heavy models excel in Free Mode, standard models need Strict Mode as risk anchor, mid-scale (35B) models show best fidelity under constraints, ultra-large (122B) models face alignment tax under rigid rules, and standard LLMs suffer a high win-rate trap that requires deep reasoning or strict guardrails to mitigate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLM-based trading agents overlook the interplay between architectural reasoning and strategy consistency.

Method: Strat-LLM framework with Stratified Strategy Alignment, operating in live-forward setting throughout 2025, integrating sequential prices, real-time news, and annual reports to eliminate look-ahead bias.

Result: (1) Reasoning-heavy models peak in Free Mode; standard models need Strict Mode. (2) Alignment utility is regime-dependent. (3) Mid-scale (35B) optimal under strict constraints; ultra-large (122B) face alignment tax under rigid rules but excel in Guided Mode. (4) Standard LLMs fall into high win-rate trap.

Conclusion: The interplay between model reasoning capability and strategy alignment mode is critical for trading performance, with recommendations for different model scales and market regimes.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving into autonomous trading agents, yet existing benchmarks often overlook the interplay between architectural reasoning and strategy consistency. We propose Strat-LLM, a framework grounded in Stratified Strategy Alignment. Operating in a live-forward setting throughout 2025, it integrates heterogeneous data including sequential prices, real-time news, and annual reports to eliminate look-ahead bias. Extensive stress tests on A-share and U.S. markets reveal: (1) reasoning-heavy models achieve peak utility in Free Mode via internal logic, whereas standard models require Strict Mode as a vital risk anchor; (2) alignment utility is regime-dependent, with Free and Guided modes capturing momentum in uptrending markets, while Strict Mode mitigates drawdowns in downtrends; (3) mid-scale models (35B) show optimal fidelity under strict constraints, whereas ultra-large models (122B) suffer an alignment tax under rigid rules but gain a performance premium in Guided Mode; (4) standard LLMs often fall into a high win-rate trap, optimizing for small gains at the expense of total returns, which can only be mitigated through deep reasoning or strict external guardrails. Project details are available at https://Strat-LLM.github.io.

</details>


### [108] [Pathways to AGI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06029)
*Gordon Fletcher,Saomai Vu Khan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper critically examines the socio-technical pathways that led to current dominant generative AI tools, identifies key leverage nodes and dead ends, compares trajectories across proprietary, open-weight, and sovereign models, analyzes alternative projects, and proposes development programs toward AGI-adjacent capabilities that prioritize transparency, wellbeing, and sustainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge deterministic assumptions about AI development by showing how current commercial AI systems are tightly linked to specific social, political, and economic circumstances, and to recover alternative pathways that were marginalized or abandoned.

Method: Critical software studies perspective; analysis of critical pathways, leverage nodes (small changes with large downstream effects), dead ends, and comparative trajectories across three foundational-model types (frontier proprietary, open-weight, domain/sovereign models).

Result: Identification of key decision points that shaped dominant generative AI ecosystems, characterization of path dependencies and alternative projects that succeeded, stalled, failed, or were absorbed.

Conclusion: Offers recommendations for socio-technical development programs that could pursue AGI-adjacent capabilities under constraints of transparency, moderation, wellbeing, and sustainable business models.

Abstract: Our focus are five related questions that stem from a critical software studies perspective. Underpinning this view is the acknowledged need to avoid assumptions regarding the inevitability of the current situation relating to AI. What we need to see is the closeness of the linkage between current commercial AI development and our prevailing social, political and economic circumstances. This does mean that the perspectives presented here are done so critically and conditionally. Most importantly, Artificial General Intelligence (AGI) is seen as being problematic both conceptually and definitionally. This conditioning of any view regarding AGI does lead the discussion in specific directions and to certain conclusions regarding the future. However, adopting this perspective enables the work to offer some final recommendations. We set out to ask the following questions, 1. What are the critical pathways that produced the current dominant generative AI tools (capabilities, product forms, adoption patterns)? 2. Which decision points acted as leverage nodes (small changes that had large downstream effects), and which dead ends reveal alternative possibilities that did not become dominant? 3. How do pathways differ across three foundational-model trajectories such as the frontier proprietary models, open-weight models or specific domain and sovereign models? 4. Which alternative projects branched from key leverage nodes, what is their current state, and why did some succeed, stall, fail or become absorbed? 5. Based on this analysis, what socio-technical development programmes could plausibly move toward AGI-adjacent capability while meeting requirements for transparency, moderation, wellbeing and sustainable business models?

</details>


### [109] [Novelty-based Tree-of-Thought Search for LLM Reasoning and Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06040)
*Leon Hamm,Zlatan Ajanovic*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novelty-based pruning method for tree-of-thought reasoning in LLMs, using novelty estimation via prompting to reduce token costs and improve search efficiency while maintaining performance on planning and reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs still struggle with robust reasoning and planning, often incurring high time and token costs. Inspired by width-based search in planning, the authors aim to transfer the concept of novelty to language domains to improve tree-of-thought reasoning efficiency.

Method: The paper proposes a measurable concept of novelty that quantifies the uniqueness of a new node (thought) relative to previously seen nodes in the search tree. Novelty is estimated by prompting an LLM, leveraging its pre-trained knowledge. This metric is used to prune branches and reduce search scope, thereby lowering overall token costs despite introducing more prompts per state.

Result: The procedure was tested and compared on several benchmarks in language-based planning and general reasoning. The method reduces overall token cost by pruning and reducing tree size.

Conclusion: Novelty-based pruning can improve the efficiency of tree-of-thought reasoning in LLMs by reducing token costs while maintaining effectiveness on planning and reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Although advances such as chain-of-thought, tree-of-thought or reinforcement learning have improved the performance of LLMs in reasoning and planning tasks, they are still brittle and have not achieved human-level performance in many domains, and often suffer from high time and token costs. Inspired by the success of width-based search in planning, we explore how the concept of novelty can be transferred to language domains and how it can improve tree-of-thought reasoning. A tree of thoughts relies on building possible "paths" of consecutive ideas or thoughts. These are generated by repeatedly prompting an LLM. In our paper, a measurable concept of novelty is proposed that describes the uniqueness of a new node (thought) in comparison to nodes previously seen in the search tree. Novelty is estimated by prompting an LLM and making use of embedded general knowledge from pre-training. This metric can then be used to prune branches and reduce the scope of the search. Although this method introduces more prompts per state, the overall token cost can be reduced by pruning and reducing the overall tree size. This procedure is tested and compared using several benchmarks in language-based planning and general reasoning.

</details>


### [110] [Visual Fingerprints for LLM Generation Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06054)
*Amal Alnouri,Andreas Hinterreiter,Christina Humer,Furui Cheng,Marc Streit*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces visual fingerprints—distribution-level visualizations of linguistic choices (content, expression, structure)—to compare LLM outputs across different generation conditions (prompts, system instructions, model parameters, architecture).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how different generation conditions (prompts, system instructions, model parameters, architecture) shape LLM behaviors is essential for prompt design and model evaluation, but remains challenging due to the stochastic and open-ended nature of text generation.

Method: Model responses as collections of linguistic choices (content, expression, structure), extract these using NLP pipelines, represent their distributions across repeated samples, and visualize them as visual fingerprints for distribution-level comparison.

Result: Through four usage scenarios, visual fingerprints reveal consistent patterns in LLM behavior that are difficult to observe through individual responses or aggregate metrics.

Conclusion: Visual fingerprints enable direct, distribution-level comparison of condition-specific tendencies in LLM outputs, providing a novel approach to understanding generation condition biases.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) outputs arise from complex interactions among prompts, system instructions, model parameters, and architecture. We refer to specific configurations of these factors as generation conditions, each of which can bias outputs in various ways. Understanding how different generation conditions shape model behaviors is essential for tasks such as prompt design and model evaluation, yet it remains challenging due to the stochastic and open-ended nature of text generation. We present an approach to visually compare LLM outputs across generation conditions by modeling responses as collections of linguistic choices, including content, expression, and structure. We extract these choices using natural language processing pipelines and represent their distributions across repeated samples. We then visualize these distributions as visual fingerprints, enabling direct, distribution-level comparison of condition-specific tendencies. Through four usage scenarios, we demonstrate how visual fingerprints reveal consistent patterns in LLM behavior that are difficult to observe through individual responses or aggregate metrics.

</details>


### [111] [VibeServe: Can AI Agents Build Bespoke LLM Serving Systems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06068)
*Keisuke Kamahori,Shihang Li,Simon Peter,Baris Kasikci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VibeServe is an agentic loop that automatically synthesizes bespoke LLM serving systems for different usage scenarios, generating entire serving stacks end-to-end. It uses inner/outer loops to search over system designs, and remains competitive with vLLM in standard settings while outperforming existing systems in non-standard scenarios involving novel architectures, workload knowledge, and hardware-specific optimizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM serving systems use a single general-purpose stack hand-tuned for every model and workload, which misses optimization opportunities in non-standard scenarios. The authors propose shifting from runtime generality to generation-time specialization.

Method: VibeServe uses a multi-agent loop with an outer loop for planning and searching over system designs, and an inner loop for implementing candidates, checking correctness, and measuring performance on target benchmarks.

Result: Competitive with vLLM in standard deployment settings, and outperforms existing systems in six non-standard scenarios involving novel model architectures, workload knowledge, and hardware-specific optimizations.

Conclusion: Generation-time specialization is a viable alternative to runtime generality for LLM serving infrastructure, suggesting a different point in the design space for infrastructure software.

Abstract: For years, we have built LLM serving systems like any other critical infrastructure: a single general-purpose stack, hand-tuned over many engineer-years, meant to support every model and workload. In this paper, we take the opposite bet: a multi-agent loop that automatically synthesizes bespoke serving systems for different usage scenarios. We propose VibeServe, the first agentic loop that generates entire LLM serving stacks end-to-end. VibeServe uses an outer loop to plan and track the search over system designs, and an inner loop to implement candidates, check correctness, and measure performance on the target benchmark. In the standard deployment setting, where existing stacks are highly optimized, VibeServe remains competitive with vLLM, showing that generation-time specialization need not come at the cost of performance. More interestingly, in non-standard scenarios, VibeServe outperforms existing systems by exploiting opportunities that generic systems miss in six scenarios involving non-standard model architectures, workload knowledge, and hardware-specific optimizations. Together, these results suggest a different point in the design space for infrastructure software: generation-time specialization rather than runtime generality. Code is available at https://github.com/uw-syfi/vibe-serve.

</details>


### [112] [Safety Certification is Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06087)
*Oliver Schön,Licio Romao,Sadegh Soudjani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a kernel embedding framework for safety certification of dynamical systems that directly estimates T-step safety probability from trajectory data as a classification problem, avoiding compounding errors from recursive dynamic programming approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DP-based safety certification methods suffer from compounding errors in safety probability estimates as the horizon T grows, leading to vacuous lower bounds. Additionally, they rely on Markovian assumptions that may not hold in practice.

Method: Treats safety certification as a classification problem on trajectory data using a kernel embedding framework, directly estimating the T-step safety probability without recursion. This subsumes existing approaches (barrier certificates, robust Markov models) as special cases.

Result: Direct estimators remain stable independent of the certification horizon and in non-Markovian settings, while DP-based certificates become unsound. Validated on a neural-controlled quadrotor simulation.

Conclusion: The kernel embedding framework bypasses compounding errors across horizons and enables certification for non-Markovian dynamics, overcoming key limitations of recursive DP-based methods.

Abstract: The goal of this paper is certifying safety of dynamical systems subject to uncertainty. Existing approaches use trajectory data to estimate transition probabilities, and compute safety probabilities recursively via dynamic programming (DP). This recursion may lead to compounding errors in the certified safety probability, thus collapsing to a vacuous lower bound for growing horizons $T$. We propose a kernel embedding framework that treats safety certification as a classification problem on trajectory data, directly estimating the $T$-step safety probability without recursion. We show that the framework subsumes well-established approaches from the literature (e.g., barrier certificates, robust Markov models) as special cases, and allows us to go beyond their limitations. As the main consequence, it bypasses compounding error across the horizon and enables certification for systems with non-Markovian dynamics. We demonstrate that direct estimators remain stable independent of the certification horizon and in the non-Markovian setting, whilst DP-based certificates silently go unsound -- confirmed in simulation on a neural-controlled quadrotor.

</details>


### [113] [Shallow Prefill, Deep Decoding: Efficient Long-Context Inference via Layer-Asymmetric KV Visibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06105)
*Jungsuk Oh,Hyeseo Jeon,Hyunjune Ji,Kyongmin Kong,Jay-Yoon Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPEED introduces a phase-asymmetric KV-visibility policy where non-anchor prompt tokens are only kept in lower layers during Prefill, while Decode-phase tokens maintain full-depth visibility, reducing long-context inference costs with minimal quality loss (51.2 vs 51.4 average score, with 33% TTFT improvement, 22% TPOT improvement, and 25% KV memory reduction at 128K context).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context inference in decoder-only LLMs is computationally expensive because prompts undergo full-depth KV caching during Prefill and are repeatedly attended to during autoregressive Decode, leading to high latency and memory overhead.

Method: SPEED uses a phase-asymmetric KV-visibility policy: non-anchor prompt-token KV states are materialized only in lower layers during Prefill, while Decode-phase tokens retain full-depth visibility. Only a minimal Beginning-of-Sequence (BoS) anchor is kept at all layers. This removes prefill tokens from the upper-layer Decode visibility set entirely.

Result: On Llama-3.1-8B with instruction tuning, SPEED (using 75% of layers for prefill tokens) achieves 51.2 average OLMES score vs 51.4 baseline, with 33% better TTFT, 22% better TPOT, and 25.0% reduction in active KV memory at 128K context.

Conclusion: Long-context prompt tokens do not need to persist as full-depth KV-cache objects during Decode; the cutoff retains key prompt-selection and representation-stabilization regions of the full-depth model, enabling efficient long-context inference.

Abstract: Long-context inference in decoder-only language models is costly because long prompts are processed during Prefill, cached at every layer, and repeatedly attended to during autoregressive Decode. We introduce \emph{Shallow Prefill, dEEp Decode} (SPEED), a phase-asymmetric KV-visibility policy that materializes non-anchor prompt-token KV states only in lower layers while keeping Decode-phase tokens full-depth. Unlike previous approaches that make upper-layer prompt KV states cheaper to store or construct, SPEED removes prefill tokens from the upper-layer Decode visibility set altogether. With a minimal BoS anchor, this simple change preserves broad benchmark quality while reducing long-context cost. In a controlled Llama-3.1-8B instruction-tuning study, SPEED using only 75\% of layers for prefill tokens reaches 51.2 average score on OLMES-style benchmarks, compared with 51.4 for the full-depth baseline, while improving TTFT by 33\%, TPOT by 22\%, and reducing active KV memory by 25.0\% at 128K context. Layer-wise diagnostics suggest that this cutoff retains the main prompt-selection and representation-stabilization regions of the full-depth model. These results show that long-context prompt tokens need not always persist as full-depth KV-cache objects when Decode-phase tokens remain full-depth.

</details>


### [114] [On Time, Within Budget: Constraint-Driven Online Resource Allocation for Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06110)
*Xinglin Wang,Zishen Liu,Shaoxiong Feng,Peiwen Yuan,Yiwei Li,Jiayi Shi,Yueqi Zhang,Chuyi Tan,Ji Zhang,Boyuan Pan,Yao Hu,Kan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses constraint-driven online resource allocation for agentic workflows, proposing Monte Carlo Portfolio Planning (MCPP), a closed-loop planner that allocates models and parallel samples across subtasks to maximize constrained completion probability under budget and deadline constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real deployments of agentic systems impose explicit budget and deadline constraints on workflow completion, shifting the optimization goal from average efficiency to maximizing the probability of completing the entire workflow within these constraints. Existing work focuses on optimizing the performance-cost-latency frontier but does not address this constrained setting.

Method: Formulates the problem as a finite-horizon stochastic online allocation problem. Proposes Monte Carlo Portfolio Planning (MCPP), a lightweight closed-loop planner that estimates constrained completion probability via simulated workflow executions and replans after observing outcomes.

Result: On CodeFlow and ProofFlow benchmarks, MCPP consistently improves constrained completion probability over strong baselines across a wide range of budget-deadline constraints.

Conclusion: MCPP effectively addresses the practical need for agentic workflow completion under explicit resource constraints, outperforming existing approaches in constrained settings.

Abstract: Agentic systems increasingly solve complex user requests by executing orchestrated workflows, where subtasks are assigned to specialized models or tools and coordinated according to their dependencies. While recent work improves agent efficiency by optimizing the performance--cost--latency frontier, real deployments often impose concrete requirements: a workflow must be completed within a specified budget and before a specified deadline. This shifts the goal from average efficiency optimization to maximizing the probability that the entire workflow completes successfully under explicit budget and deadline constraints. We study \emph{constraint-driven online resource allocation for agentic workflows}. Given a dependency-structured workflow and estimates of success rates and generation lengths for each subtask--model pair, the executor allocates models and parallel samples across simultaneously executable subtasks while managing the remaining budget and time. We formulate this setting as a finite-horizon stochastic online allocation problem and propose \emph{Monte Carlo Portfolio Planning} (MCPP), a lightweight closed-loop planner that directly estimates constrained completion probability through simulated workflow executions and replans after observed outcomes. Experiments on CodeFlow and ProofFlow demonstrate that MCPP consistently improves constrained completion probability over strong baselines across a wide range of budget--deadline constraints.

</details>


### [115] [CrossCult-KIBench: A Benchmark for Cross-Cultural Knowledge Insertion in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06115)
*Zhen Zeng,Leijiang Gu,Feng Li,Jing Yu,Zenglin Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces cross-cultural knowledge insertion for MLLMs, a benchmark (CrossCult-KIBench) with 9,800 cases across three language-culture groups, and a baseline method (MCKI) using external memory retrieval, revealing challenges in balancing cultural adaptation with behavioral preservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs trained on English-centric data produce culturally inappropriate responses in cross-cultural settings, necessitating methods for targeted cultural adaptation without disrupting behavior in other cultures.

Method: The authors propose CrossCult-KIBench (9,800 image-grounded cases, 49 visual scenarios, 3 language-culture groups) supporting single-insert and sequential-insert evaluation, and Memory-Conditioned Knowledge Insertion (MCKI) which retrieves cultural knowledge from external memory using frozen MLLM representations as conditional prompts.

Result: Current approaches struggle to balance effective cultural adaptation with behavioral preservation on the benchmark, highlighting a key challenge in developing culturally-aware MLLMs.

Conclusion: The work underscores an important research direction for developing more culturally adaptive and responsible MLLMs through cross-cultural knowledge insertion.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), trained primarily on English-centric data, frequently generate culturally inappropriate or misaligned responses in cross-cultural settings. To mitigate this, we introduce the task of cross-cultural knowledge insertion, which focuses on adapting models to specific cultural contexts while preserving their original behavior in other cultures. To facilitate research in this area, we introduce CrossCult-KIBench, a comprehensive evaluation benchmark for assessing both the effectiveness of knowledge insertion and its unintended side effects on non-target cultures. The benchmark includes 9,800 image-grounded cases covering 49 culturally relevant visual scenarios across English, Chinese, and Arabic language-culture groups. It supports evaluation in both single-insert and sequential-insert settings. We also propose Memory-Conditioned Knowledge Insertion (MCKI) as a baseline method. MCKI retrieves relevant cultural knowledge from an external memory using frozen MLLM representations, prepending matched entries as conditional prompts when applicable. Extensive experiments on CrossCult-KIBench reveal that current approaches struggle to balance effective cultural adaptation with behavioral preservation, highlighting a key challenge in developing culturally-aware MLLMs. Our work thus underscores an important research direction for developing more culturally adaptive and responsible MLLMs.

</details>


### [116] [Back to the Beginning of Heuristic Design: Bridging Code and Knowledge with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06123)
*Nguyen Viet Tuan Kiet,Bui Dinh Pham,Dao Van Tung,Tran Cong Dao,Huynh Thi Thanh Binh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a top-down, knowledge-first paradigm for automatic heuristic design (AHD) that complements existing bottom-up code-centric approaches, formalizing it via a statistical-learning view with a distortion-compression trade-off, and demonstrating improvements in efficiency, transfer, and generalization across combinatorial optimization and beyond.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AHD methods follow a bottom-up paradigm (code → execution feedback → refinement), which is incomplete because insights remain tied to low-level implementations and are not explicitly reusable. A complementary top-down view treating knowledge as the primary search object is needed.

Method: Formalizes AHD as a statistical-learning problem with a distortion-compression trade-off; instantiates the knowledge-first paradigm in both population-based and tree-based AHD frameworks; compares against code-centric pipelines and a combined strategy.

Result: Knowledge-first search improves discovery efficiency, transferability, and generalization across CO and non-CO tasks, often outperforming code-centric pipelines; combining both strategies yields further gains.

Conclusion: Progress in AHD depends on iteratively constructing and evolving interpretable hypotheses that retain value beyond a single search trajectory.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have recently advanced automatic heuristic design (AHD) for combinatorial optimization (CO), where candidate heuristics are iteratively proposed, evaluated, and refined. Most existing approaches search over executable programs and distill insights from execution feedback to guide later iterations. Because this process moves from low-level implementations to high-level principles, we refer to it as a bottom-up paradigm. We argue that this view is incomplete and introduce a complementary top-down perspective: knowledge becomes the primary search object and code merely instantiates and tests it, making what is learned explicit and reusable across problems and trajectories. We formalize this shift through a statistical-learning view that exposes a distortion--compression trade-off, and instantiate it in both population-based and tree-based AHD frameworks. Across CO and tasks beyond it, knowledge-first search improves discovery efficiency, transfer, and generalization, often outperforming code-centric pipelines, while combining both strategies yields further gains. Our results suggest that progress in AHD depends on iteratively constructing and evolving interpretable hypotheses that retain value beyond a single search trajectory.

</details>


### [117] [P-Guide: Parameter-Efficient Prior Steering for Single-Pass CFG Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06124)
*Xin Peng,Ang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: P-Guide achieves classifier-free guidance quality in flow matching with a single inference pass by modulating only the initial latent state, reducing latency by ~50% while maintaining fidelity and prompt alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFG requires dual forward passes per sampling step, creating a significant computational bottleneck in flow matching. The goal is to achieve high-quality guidance with reduced inference cost.

Method: P-Guide modulates only the initial latent state to steer generation, avoiding explicit velocity field extrapolation during sampling. It jointly models mean and variance under both homoscedastic and heteroscedastic priors for adaptive loss attenuation.

Result: P-Guide reduces inference latency by approximately 50% while maintaining fidelity and prompt alignment competitive with standard dual-pass CFG baselines.

Conclusion: Under first-order approximation, P-Guide is equivalent to CFG in steering generation from the prior space, and heteroscedastic priors improve robustness to data uncertainty.

Abstract: Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) is essential for high-fidelity conditional generation in flow matching, yet it imposes significant computational overhead by requiring dual forward passes at each sampling step. In this work, we address this bottleneck by introducing \textbf{P-Guide}, a framework that achieves high-quality guidance through a single inference pass by modulating only the initial latent state. We further show that, under a first-order approximation, P-Guide is equivalent to CFG in the sense that it steers generation from the prior space, without requiring explicit velocity field extrapolation during sampling. We consider both homoscedastic and \textbf{heteroscedastic} priors, and find that jointly modeling the mean and variance enables adaptive loss attenuation and improved robustness to data uncertainty. Extensive experiments demonstrate that P-Guide reduces inference latency by approximately 50\% while maintaining fidelity and prompt alignment competitive with standard dual-pass CFG baselines.

</details>


### [118] [Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06130)
*Yaorui Shi,Yuxin Chen,Zhengxi Lu,Yuchun Miao,Shugui Liu,Qi GU,Xunliang Cai,Xiang Wang,An Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Skill1 trains a single policy to co-evolve skill selection, utilization, and distillation using a unified task-outcome reward signal, outperforming prior methods on ALFWorld and WebShop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods optimize skill selection, utilization, and distillation in isolation or with separate reward sources, leading to partial and conflicting evolution of these coupled capabilities in language model agents.

Method: A single policy generates a query to search the skill library, re-ranks candidates to select one, solves the task conditioned on the selected skill, and distills a new skill from the trajectory—all trained via a single task-outcome signal where low-frequency trend credits selection and high-frequency variation credits distillation.

Result: Skill1 outperforms prior skill-based and reinforcement learning baselines on ALFWorld and WebShop. Training dynamics confirm co-evolution of the three capabilities; ablations show removing any credit signal degrades evolution.

Conclusion: A unified policy trained with a single task-outcome reward that decomposes into low-frequency (selection) and high-frequency (distillation) credit signals enables effective co-evolution of skill selection, utilization, and distillation for persistent skill libraries.

Abstract: A persistent skill library allows language model agents to reuse successful strategies across tasks. Maintaining such a library requires three coupled capabilities. The agent selects a relevant skill, utilizes it during execution, and distills new skills from experience. Existing methods optimize these capabilities in isolation or with separate reward sources, resulting in partial and conflicting evolution. We propose Skill1, a framework that trains a single policy to co-evolve skill selection, utilization, and distillation toward a shared task-outcome objective. The policy generates a query to search the skill library, re-ranks candidates to select one, solves the task conditioned on it, and distills a new skill from the trajectory. All learning derives from a single task-outcome signal. Its low-frequency trend credits selection and its high-frequency variation credits distillation. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that Skill1 outperforms prior skill-based and reinforcement learning baselines. Training dynamics confirm the co-evolution of the three capabilities, and ablations show that removing any credit signal degrades the evolution.

</details>


### [119] [Graphlets as Building Blocks for Structural Vocabulary in Knowledge Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06154)
*Kossi Amouzouvi,Robert Wardenga,Jens Lehmann,Sahar Vahdati*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a model-agnostic framework using graphlets as structural tokens for Knowledge Graph Foundation Models (KGFMs), enabling transferable representations across unseen KGs. Evaluated on 51 KGs, it outperforms prior KGFMs in zero-shot inductive and transductive link prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KGFMs struggle to transfer representations across unseen KGs because, unlike language and vision, KGs lack a universal token set and have irregular, non-Euclidean topologies. The paper aims to close this gap by treating graphlets as structural tokens that recur across heterogeneous KGs.

Method: A model-agnostic framework that mines a KG between relations via pattern matching using a vocabulary of graphlets. Specifically, closed and open 2- and 3-path, and star graphlets are employed to obtain robust structural invariances.

Result: Evaluated on 51 KGs across diverse domains for zero-shot inductive and transductive link prediction. Adding simple graphlets to the vocabulary yields models that outperform prior KGFMs.

Conclusion: Graphlets serve as effective structural tokens for KGFMs, enabling transferable representations across unseen KGs by capturing recurring relational topologies, outperforming prior approaches.

Abstract: Foundation models excel at language, where sentences become tokens, and vision, where images become pixels, because both reduce to discrete symbols on a shared, fixed grid. Knowledge Graphs share the discreteness, but not the geometry. Their entities and relations are discrete symbols, yet their arrangement is relational and lacks a common, fixed grid. Knowledge Graphs (KGs) share the discreteness, but not the geometry. They form irregular, non-Euclidean topologies whose local neighborhoods differ from graph to graph. Therefore, Knowledge Graph Foundation Models (KGFMs) rely on identifying structural invariances to produce transferable representations. Without a universal token set, KGFMs are limited in their ability to transfer representations across unseen KGs. We close this gap by treating graphlets, small connected graphs, as structural tokens that recur in heterogeneous KGs. In this paper, We introduce a model-agnostic framework based on a vocabulary of graphlets that mines a KG between relations via pattern matching. In particular, we considered closed and open 2- and 3-path, and star graphlets, to obtain robust invariances. The framework is evaluated on 51 KGs from a wide range of domains, for zero-shot inductive and transductive link prediction. Experiments show that adding simple graphlets to the vocabulary yields models that outperform prior KGFMs.

</details>


### [120] [Beyond Accuracy: Policy Invariance as a Reliability Test for LLM Safety Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06161)
*Shihao Weng,Yang Feng,Xiaofei Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current LLM-as-a-Judge safety pipelines violate a basic property called policy invariance, causing verdicts to conflate agent behavior with evaluation policy wording. The paper introduces a stress-test protocol revealing that up to 9.1% of verdicts flip under content-preserving rewrites, with 18-43% of flips occurring on unambiguous cases, and proposes the Policy Invariance Score and Judge Card protocol to audit judge reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks treat LLM judge verdicts as ground-truth proxies without verifying whether those verdicts reflect actual agent behavior or merely the wording of the evaluation policy. This undermines the trustworthiness of current agent safety evaluation.

Method: The authors formalize policy invariance as three testable principles: rubric-semantics invariance, rubric-threshold invariance, and ambiguity-aware calibration. They instantiate these as a stress-test protocol applied to four agent-class judges on trajectories from ASSEBench and R-Judge.

Result: Judges respond comparably to meaningful normative shifts and meaningless structural rewrites. Content-preserving policy rewrites flip up to 9.1% of verdicts above baseline jitter, and 18-43% of all observed flips occur on unambiguous cases. An order-of-magnitude spread in judge reliability is exposed that accuracy-only leaderboards cannot capture.

Conclusion: Existing safety scores conflate what the agent did with how the evaluator was prompted. The Policy Invariance Score and Judge Card protocol are proposed to audit evaluator reliability rather than trust them by default.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-Judge pipelines have become the de facto evaluator for agent safety, yet existing benchmarks treat their verdicts as ground-truth proxies without checking whether the verdicts depend on the agent's behavior or merely on how the evaluation policy happens to be worded. We argue that any trustworthy safety judge must satisfy a basic property we call policy invariance, and we operationalize it as three testable principles: rubric-semantics invariance under certified-equivalent rewrites, rubric-threshold invariance under intentional strict-to-lenient shifts, and ambiguity-aware calibration so that verdict instability concentrates on genuinely ambiguous cases. Instantiating these principles as a stress-test protocol with four agent-class judges on trajectories drawn from ASSEBench and R-Judge, we surface a previously unmeasured failure mode: today's judges respond to meaningful normative shifts and to meaningless structural rewrites with comparable strength, and cannot tell the two apart. Content-preserving policy rewrites flip up to 9.1% of verdicts above baseline jitter, and 18-43% of all observed flips occur on unambiguous cases under such rewrites, so existing safety scores conflate what the agent did with how the evaluator was prompted. Beyond the diagnosis, we contribute the Policy Invariance Score and the Judge Card reporting protocol, which expose an order-of-magnitude spread in judge reliability that is invisible to accuracy-only leaderboards. We release the protocol and code so that future agent-safety benchmarks can audit their own evaluators rather than trust them by default.

</details>


### [121] [BioMedArena: An Open-source Toolkit for Building and Evaluating Biomedical Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06177)
*Jinge Wu,Hongjian Zhou,Mingde Zeng,Jiayuan Zhu,Junde Wu,Jiazhen Pan,Sean Wu,Honghan Wu,Fenglin Liu,David A. Clifton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BioMedArena is an open-source toolkit for evaluating foundation models as deep-research agents in biomedicine, decoupling six evaluation layers and providing 147 benchmarks + 75 tools, achieving SOTA on 8 benchmarks with +15.03% average lift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The per-paper engineering tax — inconsistent harnesses, tool registries, and model-specific integration across papers — makes fair comparison of deep research agents difficult and wastes weeks of engineering effort for each new model integration.

Method: Decouples six evaluation layers (benchmark loading, tool exposure, selection, execution mode, context management, scoring); provides 147 biomedical benchmarks, 75 tools across 9 families, 6 agent harnesses, and 6 context-management strategies; uses lightweight provider adapters for model/benchmark/tool registration.

Result: Achieves SOTA on 8 representative biomedical benchmarks with an average lift of +15.03 percentage points over prior SOTA, providing 12 backbones with competitive research capabilities.

Conclusion: BioMedArena eliminates the per-paper engineering tax and enables standardized, fair comparison of foundation models as deep-research agents in biomedicine.

Abstract: Building a deep research agent today is an exercise in glue code: the same backbone evaluated on the same benchmark can report different accuracies in different papers because harness and tool registry all differ, and integrating a new foundation model into a comparable evaluation surface costs weeks of model-specific engineering. We call this the per-paper engineering tax and release BioMedArena, an open-source toolkit that not only alleviates it but also provides an arena for fair comparison of different foundation models when evaluating them as deep-research agents. BioMedArena decouples six layers of biomedical agent evaluation -- benchmark loading, tool exposure, tool selection, execution mode, context management, and scoring -- and exposes 147 biomedical benchmarks and 75 biomedical tools across 9 functional families. Adding a new model, benchmark, or tool reduces to registering a few-line provider adapter. We further provide 6 agent harnesses with 6 context-management strategies, which provide 12 backbones with competitive research capabilities and significantly improved performance, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) results on 8 representative biomedical benchmarks, with an average lift of +15.03 percentage points over prior SOTA. The toolkit, configurations, and per-task traces are available at https://github.com/AI-in-Health/BioMedArena

</details>


### [122] [Rethinking Adapter Placement: A Dominant Adaptation Module Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06183)
*Suoxin Zhang,Run He,Di Fang,Xiang Tan,Kaixuan Chen,Huiping Zhuang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAGE identifies a single dominant adapter placement that achieves superior performance with ~0.7% of vanilla LoRA's parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LoRA methods distribute adapters broadly, but it's unknown where to place a limited number of adapters to maximize performance. This paper investigates optimal adapter placement.

Method: Introduces PAGE (Projected Adapter Gradient Energy), a gradient-based sensitivity probe to estimate trainable gradient energy per candidate LoRA adapter. Proposes DomLoRA, placing a single adapter at the identified dominant adaptation module (a shallow FFN down-projection).

Result: PAGE is highly concentrated on a single shallow FFN down-projection across model families/tasks. DomLoRA with ~0.7% of vanilla LoRA's parameters outperforms it on instruction following, math reasoning, code generation, and multi-turn conversation.

Conclusion: The dominant adaptation module is architecture-dependent but task-stable. DomLoRA improves LoRA variants, supporting dominant module placement as a practical guideline.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) is a widely used parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that places trainable low-rank adapters into frozen pre-trained models. Recent studies show that using fewer LoRA adapters may still maintain or even improve performance, but existing methods still distribute adapters broadly, leaving where to place a limited number of adapters to maximize performance largely open. To investigate this, we introduce PAGE (Projected Adapter Gradient Energy), a gradient-based sensitivity probe that estimates the initial trainable gradient energy available to each candidate LoRA adapter. Surprisingly, we find that PAGE is highly concentrated on a single shallow FFN down-projection across two model families and four downstream tasks. We term this module the dominant adaptation module and show that its layer index is architecture-dependent but task-stable. Motivated by this finding, we propose DomLoRA, a placement method that places a single adapter at the dominant adaptation module. With only ~0.7% of vanilla LoRA's trainable parameters, DomLoRA outperforms it on average across various downstream tasks, including instruction following, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and multi-turn conversation. This method also improves other LoRA variants, supporting the dominant adaptation module perspective as a practical placement guideline.

</details>


### [123] [Event-Causal RAG: A Retrieval-Augmented Generation Framework for Long Video Reasoning in Complex Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06185)
*Peizheng Yan,Yu Zhao,Liang Xie,Juntong Qi,Mingming Wang,Erwei Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Event-Causal RAG is a retrieval-augmented framework for infinite long-video reasoning that segments videos into semantically coherent events, represents them as structured State-Event-State graphs stored in a dual-store memory, and uses bidirectional retrieval to identify causal event chains for answer generation, outperforming existing methods on long-video understanding benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing large vision-language models struggle with ultra-long or infinite video reasoning due to O(n²) self-attention complexity, while current RAG approaches suffer from fragmented clip-level memory, weak temporal/causal structure modeling, and high storage and inference costs.

Method: Streaming videos are segmented into semantically coherent events, each represented as a structured State-Event-State (SES) graph capturing event context with surrounding state transitions. These graphs are merged into a global Event Knowledge Graph stored in a dual-store memory supporting both semantic matching and causal-topological retrieval. A bidirectional retrieval strategy identifies relevant event causal chains, fed with video evidence to a backbone video foundation model for answer generation.

Result: Consistently outperforms strong clip-based retrieval baselines and long-context video models on long-video understanding benchmarks, especially on multi-event integration and causal inference across long temporal gaps, while achieving improved memory efficiency and robust streaming performance.

Conclusion: Event-Causal RAG effectively addresses limitations of both end-to-end video models and existing RAG approaches for ultra-long video reasoning by leveraging event-level semantic segmentation, structured causal graph representation, and bidirectional retrieval for coherent temporal reasoning.

Abstract: Recent large vision-language models have achieved strong performance on short- and medium-length video understanding, yet they remain inadequate for ultra-long or even infinite video reasoning, where models must preserve coherent memory over extended durations and infer causal dependencies across temporally distant events. Existing end-to-end video understanding methods are fundamentally limited by the $O(n^2)$ complexity of self-attention, while recent retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) approaches still suffer from fragmented clip-level memory, weak modeling of temporal and causal structure, and high storage and online inference costs. We present Event-Causal RAG, a lightweight retrieval-augmented framework for infinite long-video reasoning. Instead of indexing fixed-length clips, our method segments streaming videos into semantically coherent events and represents each event as a structured State-Event-State (SES) graph, capturing the event together with its surrounding state transitions. These graphs are merged into a global Event Knowledge Graph and stored in a dual-store memory that supports both semantic matching and causal-topological retrieval. On top of this memory, we design a bidirectional retrieval strategy to efficiently identify the most relevant event causal chains and provide them, together with the associated video evidence, to a backbone video foundation model for answer generation. Experiments on long-video understanding benchmarks demonstrate that Event-Causal RAG consistently outperforms strong clip-based retrieval baselines and long-context video models, particularly on questions requiring multi-event integration and causal inference across long temporal gaps, while also achieving improved memory efficiency and robust streaming performance.

</details>


### [124] [OPSD Compresses What RLVR Teaches: A Post-RL Compaction Stage for Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06188)
*Jaehoon Kim,Dongha Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OPSD in thinking-enabled math reasoning primarily acts as a compression mechanism (shortening responses) rather than a correction mechanism; training on correct rollouts preserves accuracy while reducing length, but training on incorrect rollouts harms accuracy. A revised pipeline SFT→RLVR→OPSD is proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate why OPSD's accuracy gains diminish or become negative in thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning, and to understand whether OPSD functions as a compression or correction mechanism in this setting.

Method: Applied OPSD separately to correct and incorrect rollout groups to isolate compression and correction effects in thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning tasks.

Result: OPSD on correct rollouts preserves accuracy while substantially shortening responses (compression); OPSD on incorrect rollouts damages accuracy (not effective correction).

Conclusion: OPSD is more reliable as a compression mechanism than a correction mechanism for thinking-enabled math reasoning. Recommend a revised pipeline: SFT → RLVR → OPSD.

Abstract: On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD) has recently emerged as an alternative to Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), promising higher accuracy and shorter responses through token-level credit assignment from a self-teacher conditioned on privileged context. However, this promise does not carry over to thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning, where reported accuracy gains shrink and sometimes turn negative. We hypothesize that hindsight supervision can specify better token-level alternatives in short thinking-disabled outputs, but in long thinking-enabled traces it more readily identifies redundancy than supplies better replacements. To test this, we applied OPSD separately to correct and incorrect rollout groups, so that compression and correction can be observed in isolation. Our results show that in thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning, OPSD behaves most reliably as a compression mechanism rather than a correction mechanism: training only on correct rollouts preserves accuracy while substantially shortening responses, whereas training only on incorrect rollouts damages accuracy. In light of these findings, we propose a revised post-training pipeline for thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning: SFT then RLVR then OPSD.

</details>


### [125] [Systematic Evaluation of Large Language Models for Post-Discharge Clinical Action Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06191)
*Shivali Dalmia,Ananya Mantravadi,Prasanna Desikan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates zero-shot and few-shot LLMs for clinical action extraction from discharge notes using a two-stage framework, comparing them to supervised BERT models. LLMs match supervised models on binary actionability but lag on fine-grained classification. Errors stem from misalignment with annotation conventions, highlighting the need for reasoning-annotated datasets that document why spans are actionable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the viability of generative LLMs for safety-critical clinical action extraction from discharge notes without task-specific fine-tuning, under strict data-privacy constraints, with particular focus on transitions of care and post-discharge patient safety.

Method: A two-stage extraction framework that decomposes narrative discharge notes into fine-grained actionable clinical tasks via staged prompting; evaluation of zero-shot and few-shot LLMs compared against supervised BERT-based models on the CLIP discharge-note dataset.

Result: LLMs achieve comparable or superior performance to supervised models on binary actionability detection, but supervised baselines maintain a meaningful advantage on fine-grained multi-label category classification. Error analysis reveals misalignment between model reasoning and dataset annotation conventions, particularly for implicit clinical actions and rigid structural labeling rules.

Conclusion: Reported performance reflects model limitations due to lack of clinical reasoning not captured by plain annotations. Labels without rationales make it impossible to distinguish clinical reasoning failures from annotation convention mismatches. Advancing clinical NLP requires reasoning-annotated datasets that document why specific spans are actionable.

Abstract: The work in this paper evaluates zero-shot and few-shot large language models (LLMs) for safety-critical clinical action extraction using the CLIP discharge-note dataset, with particular emphasis on transitions of care and post-discharge patient safety. To manage the complexity of clinical documentation, we introduce a two-stage extraction framework that decomposes discharge notes, that are written in narrative form, into fine-grained, explicitly actionable clinical tasks through a staged prompting strategy. Our contributions include a systematic assessment of generative LLMs for clinical action extraction, a detailed comparison between general-purpose LLMs and task-specific supervised BERT-based models, and an analysis of annotation inconsistencies across different action categories. We show that contemporary LLMs achieve performance comparable to or exceeding supervised models on binary actionability detection, while supervised baselines retain a meaningful advantage on fine-grained multi-label category classification, despite the absence of task-specific fine-tuning and under strict data-privacy constraints. Qualitative error analysis reveals that many failures stem from misalignment between model reasoning and dataset annotation conventions, particularly in cases involving implicit clinical actions and rigid structural labeling rules. These results indicate that reported performance reflects model limitations due to lack of clinical reasoning, that is not captured by plain annotations. Labels without rationales make it impossible to distinguish clinical reasoning failures from annotation convention mismatches. Advancing clinical NLP requires reasoning-annotated datasets that document why specific spans are actionable, not merely which spans were labeled, enabling proper evaluation of model clinical understanding.

</details>


### [126] [The Granularity Axis: A Micro-to-Macro Latent Direction for Social Roles in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06196)
*Chonghan Qin,Xiachong Feng,Ziyun Song,Xiaocheng Feng,Jing Xiong,Lingpeng Kong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs encode social role granularity as a dominant, causally manipulable latent direction in their representation space, explaining 52.6% of variance and organizing roles from micro (individual) to macro (institutional/national) levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLM internal representations encode the granularity of social roles (from individual experiences to institutional/national reasoning) as a structured latent dimension rather than merely a stylistic surface feature.

Method: Defined a contrast-based Granularity Axis (difference between mean macro- and micro-role hidden states). Constructed 75 social roles across five granularity levels, collected 91,200 role-conditioned responses, extracted role-level hidden states, and projected them onto the axis. Validated via monotonicity, stability across layers/prompts/splits, transfer to Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and causal testing via activation steering.

Result: The Granularity Axis aligns with PC1 at cosine 0.972, explaining 52.6% of role representation variance. Role projections increase monotonically across five granularity levels, remain stable across numerous conditions, and transfer across models. Activation steering causally shifts response granularity (Llama from 2.00 to 3.17 on a five-point macro scale).

Conclusion: Social role granularity is a structured, ordered, and causally manipulable latent direction in LLM behavior, not merely a surface stylistic feature. Models differ in steering controllability depending on their default operating regime.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are routinely prompted to take on social roles ranging from individuals to institutions, yet it remains unclear whether their internal representations encode the granularity of such roles, from micro-level individual experience to macro-level organizational, institutional, or national reasoning. We show that they do. We define a contrast-based Granularity Axis as the difference between mean macro- and micro-role hidden states. In Qwen3-8B, this axis aligns with the principal axis (PC1) of the role representation space at cosine 0.972 and accounts for 52.6% of its variance, indicating that granularity is the dominant geometric axis organizing prompted social roles. We construct 75 social roles across five granularity levels and collect 91,200 role-conditioned responses over shared questions and prompt variants, then extract role-level hidden states and project them onto the axis. Role projections increase monotonically across all five levels, remain stable across layers, prompt variants, endpoint definitions, held-out splits, and score-filtered subsets, and transfer to Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. The axis is also causally relevant: activation steering along it shifts response granularity in the predicted direction, with Llama moving from 2.00 to 3.17 on a five-point macro scale under positive steering on prompts that admit local responses. The two models differ in controllability, suggesting that steering depends on each model's default operating regime. Overall, our findings suggest that social role granularity is not merely a stylistic surface feature, but a structured, ordered, and causally manipulable latent direction in role-conditioned language model behavior.

</details>


### [127] [Towards Annotation-Free Validation of MLLMs: A Vision-Language Logical Consistency Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06201)
*Ying Gu,Mei Chee Leong,Hui Li Tan,Shangbo Mao,Liyuan Li,Nancy Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Vision-Language Logical Consistency Metric (VL-LCM) to evaluate MLLMs on logical consistency without requiring ground-truth annotations, revealing that accuracy improvements do not guarantee logical consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional accuracy-based evaluation may reward unwarranted guessing in LLMs and is not suitable for novel tasks lacking ground-truth annotations, necessitating a more principled evaluation framework.

Method: Based on basic logic principles, the authors define VL-LCM to assess both sufficient and necessary cause-effect relations in vision-language tasks, applied to MC-VQA tests and NaturalBench without requiring ground-truth annotations.

Result: Evaluation of 11 recent open-source MLLMs across 4 families on MMMU and NaturalBench shows that while accuracy has improved significantly, logical consistency lags behind. VL-LCM correlates well with ground-truth-based metrics and demonstrates reliability.

Conclusion: Logical consistency, as measured by VL-LCM, can complement accuracy for both performance evaluation and reliability assessment, and it enables model selection, validation, and answer justification in tasks without ground-truth annotations.

Abstract: Dominant accuracy evaluation might reward unwarranted guessing of Large Language Models, and it might not be applicable to novel tasks for model validation without ground-truth (gt) annotation. Based on basic logic principle, we propose a novel framework to evaluate the vision-language logical consistency of MLLMs on both sufficient and necessary cause-effect relations. We define Vision-Language Logical Consistency Metric (VL-LCM) on traditional MC-VQA tests, and recent NaturalBench tests without the need for gt annotation. Through systematic experiments on representative VL benchmark MMMU and recent VL challenges like NaturalBench, we evaluated 11 recent open-source MLLMs from 4 frontier families. Our findings reveal that, despite significant progress of recent MLLMs on accuracy, logical consistency lags behind significantly. Extensive evaluations on the correlations of VL-LCM with metrics on gt, the reliability of LCM, and the relation of VL-LCM with response distribution justify the validity and applicability of VL-LCM even without gt annotation. Our findings suggest that, beyond accuracy, logical consistency could be employed for both accuracy and reliability. VL-LCM can also be employed for MLLM selection, validation, and reliable answer justification in novel tasks without gt annotation.

</details>


### [128] [Beyond Fixed Benchmarks and Worst-Case Attacks: Dynamic Boundary Evaluation for Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06213)
*Haoxiang Wang,Da Yu,Huishuai Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Dynamic Boundary Evaluation (DBE), a method to evaluate LLMs by locating each model's decision boundary (where pass probability ~0.5), placing them on a unified difficulty scale, avoiding ceiling/floor effects from fixed benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed benchmarks cause ceiling and floor effects that mask capability gaps; the most informative evaluation signal lies at the boundary where per-prompt pass probability is near 0.5.

Method: DBE consists of: (i) a calibrated item bank with per-item difficulty labels validated across 9 reference LLMs; (ii) Skill-Guided Boundary Search (SGBS), an API-level search algorithm to find boundary items for a target LLM; (iii) an adaptive evaluation protocol placing LLMs on a unified scale and expanding the bank when needed.

Result: Instantiated on four categories: harmful request refusal, over-refusal, constrained instruction following, and multi-turn sycophancy resistance. Covers a broader model spectrum without saturation and is compatible with existing datasets.

Conclusion: DBE enables more informative, adaptive LLM evaluation by actively locating each model's difficulty boundary, producing globally comparable results without ceiling or floor effects.

Abstract: Evaluating large language models (LLMs) today rests on fixed benchmarks that apply the same set of items to any model, producing ceiling and floor effects that mask capability gaps. We argue that the most informative evaluation signal lies at the boundary, where the per-prompt pass probability is near $0.5$ under random-sampling decoding, and propose Dynamic Boundary Evaluation (DBE), which actively locates each model's boundary and places it on a globally comparable difficulty scale. DBE delivers three artifacts: (i) a calibrated item bank covering safety, capability, and truthfulness, with per-item difficulty labels validated across $9$ reference LLMs; (ii) Skill-Guided Boundary Search (SGBS), a search algorithm that finds boundary items for a given target LLM using only API-level query access; and (iii) an evaluation protocol that places a new LLM on a unified ability scale and grows the evaluation set adaptively when the target falls outside the bank's coverage. We instantiate DBE on four categories spanning safety (harmful request refusal and over-refusal), capability (constrained instruction following), and truthfulness (multi-turn sycophancy resistance). The resulting evaluation covers a broader model spectrum without saturation while remaining compatible with existing datasets.

</details>


### [129] [Joint Consistency: A Unified Test-Time Aggregation Framework via Energy Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06219)
*Yunzhen Yao,Hongye Wang,Yahong Wang,Michael C. Gastpar,Bo Jiang,Lie He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Joint Consistency (JC), a constrained Ising-type energy minimization framework for test-time aggregation that combines independent evaluation signals (external fields) with pairwise comparisons (interactions) to improve reasoning accuracy, outperforming existing voting and weighted aggregation methods on math and code reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time aggregation methods rely on isolated evaluation signals or answer frequencies without considering comparative interactions among candidate reasoning traces, limiting their effectiveness.

Method: JC formulates aggregation as a constrained Ising-type energy minimization problem, where independent evaluation signals act as external fields and pairwise comparisons (via LLM-as-a-judge) act as interactions. It subsumes voting and weighted aggregation as special cases, with an efficient approximation for scalability.

Result: JC consistently outperforms existing baselines across diverse tasks, judge models, trace budgets, and trace-generation settings on math and code reasoning benchmarks.

Conclusion: Incorporating comparative interactions among candidate traces via a principled energy minimization framework improves test-time aggregation, and the method is both theoretically grounded and practically efficient.

Abstract: This paper studies test-time aggregation, an approach that generates multiple reasoning traces and aggregates them into a final answer. Most existing methods rely on evaluation signals collected from candidate traces in isolation or answer frequencies, while ignoring comparative interactions among candidates. We propose Joint Consistency (JC), formulated as a constrained Ising-type energy minimization problem, where independent evaluation signals act as external fields and pairwise comparisons act as interactions. JC provides a unified framework for test-time aggregation that subsumes existing voting and weighted aggregation methods as special cases. Our construction of the interaction matrix leverages LLM-as-a-judge comparisons, and admits a theoretical interpretation under answer-level homogeneity assumptions. Moreover, we develop an efficient approximation strategy that makes interaction modeling practical for large-scale test-time aggregation.
